```
[danger] dist player=USA-bot    n=80 cells=4168 ground min=0 median=27919 max=156348 | in units median=818  max=4582  ref=3412
[danger] dist player=Russia-bot n=80 cells=3793 ground min=1 median=94010 max=526457 | in units median=2755 max=15429 ref=3412
```

Reproducible, not a one-off: six earlier `reference` lines in the pre-run log carry the same `ground=3412`.

- **The median landed near the carbine end, which was the feared direction.** `3412` against a statically-derived carbine **2,237** and automatic rifleman **7,820** — 1.53× the carbine, 0.44× the rifleman.
- **The evac guard is below the field it gates.** `EvacDangerUnits = 50` (`SupplyFollowerBotModule.cs:106`) and `DangerUnitsToField = units × reference / 100` (`DangerFieldLayer.cs:255-265`), so evacLevel = 50 × 3412 / 100 = **1,706**. A decayed mobile contact at max range is **2,795**, so the decayed rumour alone trips evac — the reference would have to exceed **5,590** to clear it. Worse, by the code's own criterion (`DangerFieldLayer.cs:466-467`: *"a threshold the median already exceeds is firing unconditionally"*), 1,706 sits **16× below** USA-bot's live median cell and **55× below** Russia-bot's.
- **The `Desc` reasoning at `SupplyFollowerBotModule.cs:100-106` is now falsified by measurement.** It argues "50 units sits above that tail by construction" from kernel taper geometry. Measured, it sits far below the median. **Deriving a threshold from kernel geometry predicts the SHAPE of one contact's envelope and says nothing about how many envelopes overlap a cell in a real match** — that is the reasoning error, and it is the same class as the one this branch already fixed one term over.
- **The unit itself is the problem, by the code's own stated tell** (`DangerFieldLayer.cs:416-417`: *"if min and max straddle the median by more than ~2 orders, the median is a weak reference and the unit wants revisiting — not the individual thresholds"*). Measured min=164, median=3412, max=1,271,250 — the max is **2.57 orders / 372×** above the median, and only **92 of 424** types contribute at all. So this is the revisit-the-unit case, not the retune-thirteen-thresholds case.
- **Method note that generalises:** the instrumentation was written to be read by one ordinary session, and it worked — but only because it logs the SPREAD and a live DISTRIBUTION alongside the scalar. A bare `reference=3412` would have looked fine. **A calibration constant should never be logged without the spread of the population it summarises.**
- The prior `66,834` live-log median (taken while the cadence bug was live) is superseded and should not be quoted; the current equivalents are 27,919 / 94,010 per side.
- Log hygiene: `debug.log` is **rotated at game start** (observed 9,594,254 → 162,780 bytes), so a byte-offset tail captured before launch is invalid. Re-read the whole file after the run.

## 2026-08-09 — a documentation quarantine has two halves, and only one of them was liftable when the branch merged

Lifting the danger-field quarantine over `DOCS/bots/` after `auto/danger-scale` landed (`6fc1cfff` → `1092573d` → `c69835eb`, reconciled `5642d931`; worked at `main @ af36e686`). Documentation-only pass — no code touched, nothing run.

- **The reusable line is `field-side` versus `crossing`.** The quarantine had said "every danger-field magnitude is unreliable". Post-merge that is two different claims with different evidence: anything on the **field's own side** (the fire-cycle formula, per-type core intensities, per-cell steps, and the RANKING of weapon classes) is static ruleset arithmetic and re-derives from the source in minutes; anything that **crosses** from a configured constant to a field value cannot, because the denominator `DangerKernelMath.ReferenceIntensity` (`DangerFieldLayer.cs:218-234`) is a **median over every ground-threatening actor type in the ruleset**, resolved at `IWorldLoaded` (`:407-408`). **A derived unit does not make a threshold verifiable by reading — it moves the unverifiable part from thirteen constants into one denominator.** That is a large improvement and zero progress on calibration, and a doc that conflates them will over-claim.
- **Re-derivation of the three pinned intensities, third independent pass, exact:** `abrams` **521,914**, automatic rifleman (`5.56mm.AR`) **7,820**, carbine (`5.56mm.E3`) **2,237** — matching the hand transcription and `DangerFieldKernelTest` (`:111-150`, `:186-190`). Two more computed the same way and *not* test-pinned: `bmp2` **184,966**, `AT`/`ATGM` **7,560**. Executed ranking is now `abrams` › `bmp2` › `AR` › `AT` › `e3`: **armour above infantry**, an Abrams ≈2.8× a BMP2 and ≈67× a rifleman.
- **The pre-fix inversion, stated precisely because two docs had it backwards.** `04` §3.2's old headline (Abrams intensity `67,850,000`) was exact arithmetic against code that evaluated in `int`: `2,300,000 × 2,950 ≈ 6.79e9` wrapped negative, fell through the `< 1` guard and clamped to **1**, while a BMP2's product (`1,440 × 1,526 ≈ 2.2e6`) did not wrap. **So the field as executed ranked a BMP2 ~22,000× ABOVE an Abrams while the doc concluded it over-ranked armour.** A magnitude computed in exact arithmetic and a magnitude computed in the code's own integer types are different claims; a doc that publishes the first while describing the second inverts its own finding.
- **A dated snapshot gets superseded, a reference doc gets corrected — and the tell is whether the doc's header names a commit.** `DOCS/bots/05` is headed "Reconciled against `main @ 25a8aebd`", so its stale danger banner was **superseded by a new banner above the body**, not edited away; `04`/`06`/`README` carry corrections in place at the point each claim occurs. Rewriting a dated snapshot's body destroys the only record of what was believed when it was taken.

## 2026-08-09 — a map decoder has an INDEPENDENT ORACLE checked into the repo beside it, and the metric that makes the oracle usable is the one that throws away most of the pixels

From `auto/nav-guard` (base `af36e686`), building `tools/nav-guard/` — a static per-locomotor connectivity gate — and reproducing the 335-cell river-zeta pocket that `dd3430a8` attributes to the reverted diagonal-squeeze rule `b164a312`.

- **`map.png` is a second, independent rendering of `map.bin` produced by the engine itself (`Map.SavePreview`, `Map.cs:1222`), and it is the only reason a hand-written decoder can be trusted.** A wrong terrain decode does not look wrong downstream: a transposed tile plane (both planes are stored **column-major**, `for i in X: for j in Y`, `Map.cs:414-449`) or an off-by-one still yields a plausible component count, and nothing downstream contradicts it. Rendering the decode and diffing it against the checked-in preview turns "a number from an unvalidated parser" into a pinned claim. Both bugs it caught were invisible in the connectivity output: the preview keeps the **first** actor on a stacked cell (`positions.GroupBy(...).ToDictionary(g => g.Key, g => g.First().Color)`, `Map.cs:1286-1288`) where I kept the last, and `BlocksDiagonalSqueeze` tagging was being skipped on terrain the locomotor could not cross — silently dropping 1 of river-zeta's 25 traps as a shoulder, in a model whose entire subject is shoulders.
- **The naive metric is useless and the useful one discards most of the image.** Comparing every pixel scores river-zeta at **38.46%**, because ~3,100 of its 8,036 cells carry a `v16`/`v17`/`rice` field actor the preview paints over the terrain (`AppearsOnMapPreview`). That number says nothing about the decoder. Restricting agreement to cells where **neither side involves an actor colour** isolates tile→terrain-type→colour and reads **100.00% on all ten maps**. Generalises past this tool: when validating one layer against a composite rendering, score only the subset that layer owns, or the layers you are not testing dominate the result.
- **"Explained" must be a named, counted bucket with an independent check, or it is just a tolerance with better manners.** Every remaining mismatched pixel is classified — `stale` (one side has an actor the other does not) or `ore` (an RA-era resource cell still in the `map.bin` resource plane, painted when the preview was saved) — and `unexplained` is gated at zero. The ore bucket matched the non-zero resource-plane cell count **exactly** on all three affected maps (twin-rivers 38/38, x-lake 24/24, seventh-woods 8/8), and river-zeta's six stale pixels are exactly the seven `t14`/`t15` trees deleted in `0fa152f1`, whose footprint cell is offset `(1,1)`. A bucket checkable against a count computed a different way is evidence; a fudge factor named "expected variance" is not.
- **A connectivity model for this engine has to be EDGE-level, and that is not a refinement — it is the whole bug class.** The diagonal-squeeze rule denies a step between two cells that both stay individually passable: the blockage is in the two *shoulders* (`DiagonalSqueezeGeometry.Shoulders`), so a per-cell passability map cannot represent it and a flood fill over one cannot find the pocket. Any future guard over movement must model the step, not the square.
- **The reverted rule's true cost was ~2× the number the original finding reported, and the original was still the right number to report.** Under `--squeeze generic`, river-zeta vehicles lose **513–619** cells from the largest component (components 51 → 164). The 335-cell hedged field is the single largest pocket; the other ~180 cells are scattered over roughly a hundred tiny ones. **Report the excursion a human can see and confirm, not the aggregate** — the same lesson as the truck-churn amplitude note (2026-08-08 below), reached from the opposite direction.
- **The shipped `tagged` rule changes no connectivity metric on any map, and the reason is a property of the maps, not of the rule.** It is not inert: it denies exactly the 13 diagonal steps between river-zeta's 13 corner-to-corner trap pairs (25 traps, matching `dd3430a8`'s count). But all 13 gaps already hold a `barb` actor and **no locomotor lists `barbedwire` under `Passes`**, so every denied step ran between cells no vehicle could occupy. So "the shipped fix is connectivity-safe" proves nothing about the rule in general — lay traps diagonally on open ground and it will cut reachability. **When a guard reports zero delta for a change that should have had one, check whether pre-existing state is masking it before recording the change as safe.**
- **`Locomotor` consults `Passes` and never `Crushes` when deciding whether a cell is enterable** (`IsBlockedBy`, `Locomotor.cs:434-444` — `Info.PassableClasses` only). A crush class with no matching pass class is inert for pathfinding: the unit can never enter the cell, so it never crushes what is in it. Affected ww3mod locomotors are listed in `WORKSPACE/bugs/discovered.md`.

## 2026-08-09 — the danger field's INPUT was a mis-modelled fire cycle, and the int overflow everyone (including me) reported as the bug was only its loudest symptom

From `auto/danger-scale` (base `910507c1`), implementing `WORKSPACE/recon/260809-truck-loop-from-live-log.md`. The recon's diagnosis — `EvacDangerThreshold: 60` is an RA-scale constant under a field reading orders of magnitude higher — was directionally right. Writing the regression test at real WW3MOD magnitudes then exposed an int overflow underneath it. **Review then found that the overflow was itself downstream of a third, deeper defect, and the correct order of repair is the whole lesson of this entry.**

- **`WeaponThroughput` modelled the fire cycle as `damage × Burst × window / ReloadDelay`, and that is not how this engine fires.** `Armament.CanFire` refuses while `IsReloading || IsWaitingBurst` (`Armament.cs:327`); `ReloadDelay` and `BurstWait` are decremented INDEPENDENTLY in the same tick handler (`:283-287`), so a weapon arming both waits the LONGER, never the sum. `UpdateBurst` (`:624-648`) arms the full `BurstWait` only when a burst is exhausted; `UpdateMagazine` (`:608-622`) arms `ReloadDelay` only when the MAGAZINE empties, and it runs once per SHOT (`:380`) — **so `Magazine` counts shots, and a magazine-swap delay is amortised over `Magazine` shots rather than paid every burst.**
- **The error was TWO-SIDED, which is why nothing downstream could have corrected it.** ~90% of WW3MOD weapons never set `ReloadDelay` — the mod paces fire with `BurstWait`, which `ArmamentInfo.RulesetLoaded` (`:128-129`) makes mandatory by throwing without it. Those fell to the `reload = 1` fallback and were **over-stated by their entire cycle length**: `TankRound.Abrams` (23,000 damage, `BurstWait: 130`) read 2,300,000 against a true 17,692 — **130×**. The ~14 weapons that DO set `ReloadDelay` were **under-stated**, because the formula divided by a magazine swap as if it were the shot interval and ignored `Magazine`: `5.56mm.AR` (`Magazine: 100`, `ReloadDelay: 150`, `Burst: 10`, `BurstDelays: 1`, `BurstWait: 8`) read 1,333 against a true ~6,410 — **4.8× low**. A *uniform* error cancels in the reference ratio a scale-free unit is expressed against. **A 130× over-statement on one weapon class against a 5× under-statement on another does not — it re-ranks the classes relative to each other, which is the one thing a threat field exists to get right.**
- **The overflow was a symptom wearing the costume of a cause, and I reported it as the headline.** `throughput × durabilityWeight` for an Abrams (2,300,000 × 2,950 = **6.79e9**) wrapped negative in int, fell through the `intensity < 1` guard and clamped to **the floor of 1** — so every heavy vehicle stamped an aura of 1 while a rifleman stamped ~1,300, and the field ranked a rifle squad above an armoured company. All true. But **2,300,000 was never a throughput**; with the cadence corrected the same tank reads 17,692, the product is 5.2e7, and the site sits ~41× below `int.MaxValue`. **I widened three integers to hold a number that should never have been that large.** The `long`s are kept — the inputs are data-driven and a rebalance can enlarge them — but they are a bound, not the fix, and the comments now say so. **General form: when an arithmetic overflow appears in a data-driven formula, check whether the INPUT is credible before widening the accumulator. An implausible magnitude is evidence about the model, not just about the type.**
- **Sequencing rule this cost me a review round to learn: a fix that RE-DERIVES a calibration invalidates every constant fitted to the old one, so it must land FIRST.** I fitted the new thresholds to a distribution measured from the user's play log — a log recorded while the overflow was live, i.e. with every heavy contact stamping the clamped floor. My own fix destroyed the sample I had calibrated against, and merging in that order would have taken tanks from ~4,000× too weak to ~130× too strong. **Order of repair: fix the measurement, then re-derive the reference, then set thresholds — and if the new calibration cannot be measured yet, ship provisional values that say so and instrument for the next session rather than inheriting numbers from the broken regime.**
- **A pure-math fixture whose values were chosen for readability is testing a different function than the one that ships — and a fixture rewritten to "real" numbers of a WRONG formula is worse, because it documents the defect as ground truth.** `DangerFieldKernelTest`'s original fixtures used illustrative facts 4–5 orders below real units, so the suite passed for the whole life of the defect. My replacement hard-coded `groundThroughput: 23000 * 100` with a comment asserting those were "what the field ACTUALLY holds in play" — the broken formula's output, frozen into the tests as correct. The fixtures now **transcribe real weapon PARAMETERS from the mod YAML and compute throughput through the function under test**, so a cadence regression moves every dependent assertion instead of being ratified by them.
- **Test at the magnitudes the system actually runs at, or the test cannot find this class of bug.** `DangerFieldKernelTest`'s existing fixtures used illustrative facts (throughput 30–400, health 100–1000) that are 4–5 orders below real WW3MOD units, so the whole suite passed for the entire life of the defect while the shipped field was inverted. The bug appeared within seconds of writing one fixture with real numbers. **A pure-math test fixture whose values were chosen for readability is testing a different function than the one that ships.**
- **The fix for the threshold half is a DERIVED UNIT, not a retuned constant, and the choice was between normalising the FIELD and normalising the THRESHOLDS.** Normalising the field (dividing intensity at stamp time) was rejected for three reasons: it destroys the two legitimate presence tests that depend on the raw scale (`GarrisonBotModule.MinBelievedDanger: 1` = "any believed envelope reaches here", and the air channel's literal `<= 0`), it loses integer resolution in an additive field, and — decisively — **it does not stop the bug recurring, because the next author still writes a bare integer with no unit.** Instead `DangerFieldLayer` computes a `ReferenceIntensity` at world load (the MEDIAN core intensity over every ruleset actor type that threatens the channel, at full confidence) and thresholds are expressed in **danger units where 100 = one reference contact at point-blank**. Median over TYPES rather than over live contacts on purpose: a runtime distribution collapses when the field is quiet, and a threshold relative to a collapsing distribution re-fires at ambient — the original bug wearing a new hat.
- **The enforcement that makes it stick is the RENAME plus the LINT PASS — and an earlier version of this entry got the mechanism wrong in a way worth recording.** Every level threshold was renamed to carry its unit (`EvacDangerThreshold` → `EvacDangerUnits`, 13 fields across 7 modules). I claimed this made an old name a **mod load failure**, on the assumption that `FieldLoader` rejects unknown YAML keys. **It does not.** `FieldLoader.Load` (`FieldLoader.cs:560-604`) iterates the TYPE's fields and looks each one up in the YAML dictionary; it never enumerates the YAML keys, so a key matching no field is simply never read — the field silently keeps its C# default, which is the exact failure mode the rename was supposed to prevent. The real enforcement is `Lint/CheckUnknownTraitFields.cs:85-86`, which walks the YAML nodes and errors on a field the trait does not define — but it runs only under `make test`, and it covers mod rules and map rules by different paths. **Generalises: "the loader will catch it" is worth one grep before it is load-bearing.** A rename is still the right move (it puts the unit at every call site and in the YAML), but its safety net is a lint pass someone has to run, not the game refusing to start. A `[Desc]` alone would have been ignored exactly as the previous `[Desc]` saying "on the danger-field throughput scale" was. Two thresholds were deliberately NOT converted and both are documented in place: the garrison presence test (scale-free at 1, and converting it would raise the bar by orders of magnitude) and `AirDangerSafeThreshold: 0` (the air channel provably carries no territory baseline, so literal 0 means "outside every believed AA envelope" at any scale).
- **This supersedes the scope of the 2026-08-07 "geometric contour" entry below.** That entry is right that a `GroundDanger` threshold selects a contour of a believed weapon envelope rather than a danger level, and right that every consumer's tuning was suspect. What it could not know is that the contour it selects was, for a threshold in the tens against a field in the 10⁴–10⁸, **the outermost ring of the faintest believed contact on the map** — i.e. not a contour of anything in particular. It named `PoiOffensiveBotModule`'s buckets and `GroundDangerNav`'s early-out as unaudited; the audit found **15 constant-threshold consumers**, of which the buckets were collapsing every target into the single top bucket (a uniform multiplier that ranked nothing) and `MountedTransportBotModule`'s `<= 0` standoff was falling through its whole candidate loop and walking every drop 20 cells back to the SR.
- **A DETERMINISTIC search that its caller must re-validate will stall FOREVER, not intermittently — and the guard being present is what disguises it.** `ForwardStagingMath.StagingCell` descends a frontier gradient; `SupplyFollowerBotModule.ResolveDropAnchor` then rejects the result if the mover cannot stand on it. That guard is correct and was working. But the walk is deterministic over a field that changes every 25 ticks, so a rejected cell is not a one-scan miss: it re-derives the identical unreachable cell every scan and the caller rejects it every scan. In the user's log this ran **24 consecutive scans (~2.4 min)** on cell `33,31` with drop-and-leave dark throughout. **Terrain also actively attracts the walk**: unstamped water and cliff carry no danger stamp, so they read 0 — maximally safe — and a danger-guarded descent steers *toward* them. The fix is to give the walk the same passability predicate the caller uses, over the same representative cell, so it cannot return a cell its caller is obliged to reject. **General form: when a producer is deterministic and its consumer validates the output, "reject and retry next scan" is not a retry — it is an infinite loop with a heartbeat. The validation has to be pushed into the producer, or the producer needs a fallback.**
- **"It self-gates to a no-op when logging is off, so this is free" is a claim about COST that reads as a claim about COVERAGE.** `ModularBot.ReportGateSuppressions` wrote only to `UnitLifecycleLogger`, which requires `Test.Mode` plus a separate JSONL path and never touches `debug.log`. So the order damper merged the previous day could not be observed in a real match at all, and an analysis of an ordinary play log could not distinguish "the gate suppressed nothing" from "the gate cannot report" — **opposite conclusions that produce an identical log.** Its early-out also skipped `ResetSuppressions()`, so with logging off the counters accumulated for the whole match and the first `Test.Mode` window would have reported a match-long total as one window's worth. When auditing whether a shipped lever works, first check that its instrumentation is reachable from the configuration it actually ships in.

## 2026-08-08 — pick a damper by asking WHICH TRANSITION IS IRREVERSIBLE, not by copying the nearest existing one; and "value band vs dwell" is only a real choice when the signal is a scalar at all

From `auto/truck-churn` (base `a77cac90`), acting on `WORKSPACE/recon/260808-order-churn-census.md` §3.2/§3.3.

- **A hysteresis band needs ONE scalar to band. `residueUnusable` does not have one.** The latch is fed by `SupplyProvider.ResidueVerdict`, whose two informative inputs are booleans produced by a *set scan* over an aura (`FindGreatestNeedTarget`, `SupplyProvider.cs:366-436`): aura membership, `need >= MinNeedThreshold`, and affordability (`currentSupply >= pool.SupplyValue`). Three independent axes, so a band on any one leaves the other two undamped. The project's earlier "dwell beats band because the quantity is coarsely quantised" argument (the evac damper note in `ai.yaml`) does apply to the affordability axis — supply moves in `SupplyValue` steps of 5–200 out of 750, so any safe band is sub-quantum — but the **general** reason is prior to it: before asking "band or dwell", check the signal is a scalar. A boolean emitted by a set-membership scan admits only a time bound, and a time bound is the only instrument that is scale-free across all three axes at once.
- **Banding the aura would have re-opened a deliberately closed bug.** The obvious "widen the exit radius" band is specifically forbidden here: `SupplyProvider.InAuraRange` (`:927-930`) was extracted so selection and delivery agree *exactly* on the boundary (`DOCS/reference/economy.md`), and an enter/exit split re-introduces the disagreement that fix removed. **A damper applied to a predicate that an earlier fix deliberately made sharp is a regression wearing the costume of an improvement.**
- **Choose a damper's asymmetry from the COST OF EACH ERROR, and re-derive it per site.** `SupplyLogisticsMath.EvacuateWithDwell` damps *leaving* an evac and never *entering* one, because a delayed withdrawal kills the truck. The residue latch needs the mirror image — damp *setting*, never *clearing* — via the same principle, not the same direction: a spurious set sends the truck map-edge-ward and recovery is owned by a 150-tick bot scan (~9 s wasted), while a spurious clear costs one 7-tick scan of serving nobody. See the correction bullet below on why "irreversible" was the wrong word for it. Copying a sibling damper's polarity instead of re-deriving it inverts the safety property while looking consistent.
- **Rank a fix's visibility by AMPLITUDE, not by the cadence of the code it touches.** I first ranked the latch fix above the follow-Move fix because "it gates a per-tick consumer". That over-describes it: `RotateToEdge` is per-tick *checked* but `IsIdle`-*gated*, and the truck's return to the roster is sampled only at the bot's 150-tick scan, so the latch fix's visible cadence is ~150 ticks too — the same as the other. What actually separates them is **excursion size**: a latch blip sends the truck map-edge-ward and it stays gone until a scan notices the clear and yanks it back (large, and the user can name it), while an undamped follow Move is a momentary stop-and-re-accelerate (small, and describes as "smoother"). **State a user-facing prediction in terms of the excursion, because that is the thing a player can confirm or refute** — here, "trucks stop periodically wandering off toward the map edge and being dragged back", not "trucks move more smoothly".
- **A per-tick consumer behind a per-scan producer is still the shape to grep for**, with the amplitude caveat above: a decision re-checked every tick, gated on a latch re-decided every 7 ticks, with recovery owned by a module scanning every 150. Departure was detected at 0.42 s; return at 9 s. Damping applied on the slow side alone cannot shrink an excursion the fast side starts, which is why several previous truck fixes — all on the 150-tick module — did not move the symptom.
- **"Mirror, don't pin" is this project's settled answer to untestable engine state, and it should be labelled as a mirror.** Nothing under `engine/OpenRA.Test/` constructs a `World`, an `Actor` or calls `CreateActor` — which is why the repo carries ~10 `*Math` classes. Where a property genuinely cannot be reached (here `SupplyProvider.CountsAsEmpty`, an instance property), the established move is to re-implement the expression in the test with a `// Mirrors ...` note, as `DropsSupplyCacheTest` does. **A mirror pins the RULE and not the CODE, so say so in the test body**; the failure to avoid is the tautology that *looks* like a pin — I initially "defended" the empty-truck-not-stranded property by asserting a verdict that is never consulted for a drained truck, since `Tick` early-returns before `UpdateTarget`.
- **A comment asserting irreversibility is worth checking against the gate, not the intent.** I justified the damper's asymmetry with "setting the latch sells the truck and no later scan can undo it". False: `RotateToEdge` is queued only while the truck is idle, and a cleared latch puts it back on the roster where the next Move cancels the drive — that reversal *is* the wiggle being fixed. The asymmetry was still right, on **cost** (a spurious set wastes ~9 s of driving the wrong way; a spurious clear wastes one 7-tick scan). **Derive a damper's polarity from the cost of each error and re-derive it per site; copying a neighbouring damper's direction inverts the safety property while looking consistent.**
- **`git checkout -- <file>` to undo a test mutation destroys an uncommitted fix in the same file.** Verifying a counterfactual by mutating source and reverting is correct practice, but with `git checkout` on a file whose fix is not yet committed it reverts to HEAD and throws the work away. Commit first, or mutate and restore in place.

## 2026-08-08 — the reserve that "owns idle units" cannot own a whole unit CLASS, and the class it cannot own is the one the bug was reported about

From `auto/unit-purpose` (base `5be3b98b`), acting on `WORKSPACE/recon/260808-unit-purpose-census.md`. The recon's remedy — make `PoiOffensiveBotModule.StageFreePool` reachable by falling through the `targets.Count == 0` early return (`:1261-1272`) — is correct in itself and I landed it, but it **cannot fix the reported technician**, and the reason generalises.

- **`StageFreePool` stages `BuildFreePool()` (`:1908`), which filters through `IsEligibleCombatUnit` (`:2282`) — and under `UseUnitRoles: true` (set on `@experimental`, `ai.yaml:370`) that method's LAST word is `role == MainBattle || role == IndirectFire` (`:2380`).** `tecn` resolves `CaptureSpecialist` (`UnitRoleResolver.cs:347-350`, already pinned in `UnitRoleResolverTest`). So a capturer is not in the free pool at any point on the offense path, in any game state, with or without targets. Unblocking the gate changes what happens to the ground line and to artillery; it changes **nothing at all** for the unit in the screenshot.
- **The general shape: "the module that owns idle units" is only ever the owner of the ROLES it recruits.** A reserve/free-pool/catch-all built on a role-filtered recruit predicate has a silent hole exactly the size of the roles it excludes — and those are precisely the specialist classes (capturers, logistics, SHORAD) that no *other* module wants either, so they are the units most likely to go idle in the first place. Before naming a module as the catch-all for an idle unit, resolve that unit's role and check it against the recruit filter. Reading the method that *stages* is not enough; the exclusion is two calls away, in the pool builder.
- **Corollary — the real owner is the module that already names the unit.** `CaptureCoordinatorBotModule` recruits by `CapturingActorTypes` / role `CaptureSpecialist`, so it is the only module that can ever order a `tecn`; the recon's §4.2 table shows every other module excludes it explicitly. Its `QueueCaptureOrdersFromPoiMap` simply **discarded** the undispatched remainder of `available` — no order, no claim, no log — which is what left the unit `IsIdle` and unclaimed in both registries, i.e. exactly the pool `GarrisonBotModule` recruits from. **A module that filters a pool down to what it can use owes a disposition to the remainder, or it is manufacturing the idle units some other module will misuse.**
- **`RetreatCapturerWhenDone`'s own `[Desc]` (`CaptureCoordinatorBotModule.cs:282`) already stated the conclusion and got the REASON wrong**: it says a CaptureSpecialist "has no `AttackBase`, so it is EXCLUDED from every combat free pool". A `tecn` inherits `AttackFrontal` + a pistol from `^ArmedCivilian` (`infantry.yaml:349-351`), so it passes `IsEligibleCombatUnit`'s trait test and is excluded one line later, by ROLE. Right answer, wrong mechanism — and the wrong mechanism is what makes the exclusion look accidental and reversible when it is neither.
- **A gate on "has the field been built yet?" fails OPEN for the opening window — which is exactly when opening-play bugs live.** `DangerFieldLayer` creates a player's `PlayerField` lazily inside `RecomputePlayer` (`:315-316`), first reached after the deterministic `UpdateInterval/3` stagger (`:292`) and then only on that player's round-robin slot. `GarrisonBotModule` scans on **tick 1** (`scanCountdown` is a default `int` = 0). So gating it on field EXISTENCE would have left the module ungated for the first several ticks and reproduced the reported bug verbatim. The gate must instead ask `InfluenceStack.Participates(player)` — a property knowable at tick 0 — and let the reading itself return 0, which for a participant with no field yet is the honest answer ("nothing believed here"). **General form: for an availability conjunct, prefer the predicate that is true from tick 0 over the one that becomes true when the data arrives; the second silently disables the gate over exactly the warm-up window.**
- **`DOCS/reference/influence-stack.md` §Invariants "do NOT gate on `Participates`" is now in tension with `CLAUDE.md` and needs a curation ruling.** That invariant's sole stated objection is that `Participates` returns true for `@stable` and so "does *not* preserve byte-identity" — but `CLAUDE.md` (as of `875c93c1`) settles that `@stable` **inherits improvements and is never gated off on purpose**, which makes reaching `@stable` the desired outcome rather than the defect. The invariant remains correct for its original narrow case (a lever that must not move the benchmark control silently); it is wrong as a blanket prohibition. Flagged rather than edited — `DOCS/reference/` is curated and this is a policy call, not a verifiable code error.
- **A bot CAN order a still-neutral actor, and the reason is `ClientIndex`, not ownership.** `Unload` has to be issued to the BUILDING (the passengers are out of the world and unorderable), so "the house is Neutral, not ours" looks like a hard blocker. It is not. `ValidateOrder.OrderValidation` (`ValidateOrder.cs:32-49`) compares `order.Subject.Owner.ClientIndex` against the sending client id — and a **map player such as Neutral is assigned the ADMIN client's index** (`Player.cs:191`, with a `TODO: fix this` on the line), while bot orders ride that same admin/local stream. The ids match, so the order validates against an unowned actor. **This corrects an earlier version of this entry** which claimed the un-garrison path existed only because `GarrisonManager.DynamicOwnership` flips the house to the entering soldier's owner. That flip is real (`GarrisonManager.cs:89`, default true and unoverridden) and remains a clean positive detector for "did it garrison" in a test — but it is **not** what makes the order legal, and building on the wrong dependency would have made the whole release path look contingent on a trait that could be switched off. **General form: when an order unexpectedly validates, read `ValidateOrder`'s actual comparison before attributing it to ownership — `ClientIndex` is not owner identity, and for map players it is not even distinct.**
- **A bookkeeping set written at ORDER time and read by a RELEASE pass reintroduces the bug it fixes, inside one scan interval.** The release pass here tracked buildings from the moment the `EnterTransport` was queued. Between that scan and the unit's arrival the building is empty, so a release firing in that window queues an `Unload` that `Cargo` silently drops (`CanUnload` requires `!IsEmpty()`, `Cargo.cs:265-271`) and then forgets the building — after which the unit walks in and nothing can ever get it out. The remedy is not a guard but a **change of meaning**: derive the tracked set from observed OCCUPANCY each scan (`Cargo.Passengers` ∪ `GarrisonManager.PortStates[].DeployedSoldier`, and both halves are needed — a port-deployed soldier is in-world and is NOT a cargo passenger). Then "still walking" is simply "not tracked yet", and an ineffective `Unload` is re-issued next scan instead of being lost. **General form: if a set is written on INTENT and read to decide COMPLETION, the gap between the two is a live window for exactly the failure the reader exists to prevent — derive it from observed state instead.**
- **`GarrisonManager`'s `Unload` handler never reverted building ownership on the port path.** `ResolveOrder("Unload")` (`Garrison/GarrisonManager.cs:1338`) revokes each port condition and clears `DeployedSoldier`, but never called `CheckOwnershipAfterExit()` — whose only callers were the two `OnPassengerExited` paths and the dead-soldier sweep. Port soldiers are already in-world, so no `Cargo` exit fires for them, and `UnloadCargo`'s own revert never runs when the shelter is empty. A building emptied purely through ports therefore stayed owned by the garrisoning player permanently. Latent until this branch, which is the first code in the repo to issue `Unload` at a garrison building. **It is not cosmetic: owned buildings feed `ControlField` territory classification, which feeds `ProjectTerritoryBaseline`, which is the believed danger field the garrison gate itself reads** — a self-reinforcing loop where releasing a garrison would raise the very signal that justifies garrisoning. Fixed by calling `CheckOwnershipAfterExit()` after the port loop; it is safe there because it keeps ownership while any occupant remains and each later shelter exit re-runs it.

## 2026-08-08 — two engine mechanisms that make "obvious" activity reasoning wrong: `SetCenterPosition` moves an actor WITHOUT moving its cell, and `Activity.Cancel` tests `IsInterruptible` on the PARENT, not the child

Found by adversarial review of `auto/evac-polish` (`bd3abacf..418e9c60`), which refuted two load-bearing comments I had written from design intent. Both generalise far past evacuation.

- **`Drag` (and anything else driving `IPositionable.SetCenterPosition`) moves the WORLD POSITION only — the actor's CELL does not follow.** `Mobile.SetCenterPosition` (`Mobile.cs:540-553`) assigns `CenterPosition` and fires notifications but never calls `SetLocation` (`:591-610`); `Actor.Location` is `OccupiesSpace.TopLeft` = `Mobile.ToCell` (`:297`). **So an actor can be rendered several cells outside `Map.Bounds` while `Actor.Location` is still a valid in-bounds cell.** Reviewer verified no `INotifyCenterPositionChanged` implementor re-syncs the cell.
- **That single fact cuts both ways, which is why it is worth remembering as a pair.** It is what makes driving a ground unit off the map SAFE — every `CPos`-keyed consumer (shroud, influence, pathing, terrain lookups) is safe *by construction* rather than by guard, and the `WPos`-keyed ones (ActorMap bins, MapLayers vision) are clamped anyway. **And it is what made two of my comments false**: (a) a predicate testing `self.Location` to detect "has left the map" is DEAD CODE, because `Location` never leaves; (b) the unit does not stop occupying cells on the way out — `AddInfluence`/`RemoveInfluence` only re-run on `SetLocation` (`Mobile.cs:638-648`), so it **keeps blocking the cell it left from** for the whole leg. I had asserted the exact opposite ("blocks nothing on its way out").
- **`Activity.Cancel` checks `IsInterruptible` on ITSELF, not on its child (`Activity.cs:197-208`) — so a non-interruptible child can be silently orphaned by an interruptible parent.** Trace: parent `Cancel` → `ChildActivity?.Cancel(self)` → child returns immediately because `IsInterruptible = false`, staying **Active** → parent sets `State = Canceling` anyway → the parent's own `if (IsCanceling) return true;` drops the still-running child. **A child that refuses interruption does NOT protect the composition; it only protects itself.** If a parent's cleanup depends on a child completing, the PARENT must set `IsInterruptible = false`. I had reasoned that `Drag.IsInterruptible = false` made the enclosing `RotateToEdge` uncancellable, and it does not.
- **The method lesson, and it is the same one twice: a comment asserting a MECHANISM is a claim about code someone else wrote, and it decays exactly like a memory does.** Both wrong comments were written from what the design intended and never re-checked against the trait doing the work. A unit test does not catch this — 4 of the 8 pins I added tested a boundary predicate *in isolation*, correctly, while no production caller could ever satisfy it (the drive distance and the clearance margin were measured from different origins, cell-centre vs exclusive bound, so the predicate needed one more cell than the drive ever produced). **A pure-math pin proves the arithmetic, never that the caller can reach it.** Where a helper exists solely to be called from one place, pin the CALLER's numbers, or check the reachability by hand.

## 2026-08-08 — evacuation is three separate concerns wearing one name, and each was broken in a different place: the exclusion that protects an evac is not general, the crew the AI fights with are not classified, and a ground unit cannot leave the map at all

From PIPELINE items 36 and 38 on `auto/evac-polish` (base `bd3abacf`). Code-read + build + unit tests; one live run (`test-offense-ammo-guard`) whose RED is **not attributed** — see the last bullet.

- **A ground unit CANNOT path off the map, and this is structural, not a tuning issue.** `Locomotor.MovementCostForCell` returns the unreachable cost for every cell where `!world.Map.Contains(cell)` (`Locomotor.cs:191-193`), so every `Mobile` pathfind terminates at the playable boundary by construction. Aircraft escape this only because `Fly` takes a `WPos` and never consults the locomotor (`RotateToEdge.cs:184-187`). **So "make ground units drive off like aircraft do" is not a matter of picking a farther destination — the pathfinder will refuse it.** The only way out is to stop asking the pathfinder: `Drag` drives `IPositionable.SetCenterPosition` directly (`Drag.cs:52-58`).
- **Aircraft DO already fly off-map — the claim in item 38 was correct.** `RotateToEdge` pushes their despawn point 5 cells past the boundary (`ComputePastEdgePos`, `:122`) and disposes on `IsClearOfMapEdge(self, 5)` (`:164`); `FlyOffMap` independently waits for `!Map.Contains(self.Location)` (`FlyOffMap.cs:116`). Ground had no equivalent leg.
- **The ground sell was several cells INSIDE the map, which is worse than the reported "vanish at the edge cell".** The old trigger was `IsNearMapEdge(self, 4)` (`RotateToEdge.cs:201`) and the move that preceded it was `MoveTo(edgeCell, nearEnough: 2)`. So a vehicle could stop ~2 cells short of the edge cell, satisfy a predicate with a 4-cell margin, and disappear in open ground. **Anyone reading the old code as "sells at the boundary" is reading the intent, not the arithmetic.**
- **Positioning an actor outside the playable bounds is SAFE, and every layer that could have thrown is independently guarded.** `ActorMap.AddInfluence` skips cells the layer does not contain (`CellLayer.Contains` → `Bounds.Contains(u,v)`, false for negatives) so an off-map unit simply occupies nothing; `BinRectangleCoveringWorldArea` clamps bin indices to `[0, cols-1]`/`[0, rows-1]` (`ActorMap.cs:620-624`) so negative world coordinates cannot index out of range. **This matters more than it looks: test maps here are authored with `Bounds: 0,0,66,34`, i.e. ZERO border**, so "outside the playable area" can also mean outside `MapSize` — and it still does not throw.
- **`Drag` is `IsInterruptible = false`, and that single fact is what makes an off-map leg safe to start.** `Activity.Cancel` returns false without setting `Canceling` when a child refuses interruption, so once the drive-off begins the parent `RotateToEdge` cannot be cancelled and the unit cannot be stranded outside the map by a competing order. The converse risk is the one that needs a deadline: `Drag.Tick` returns without advancing while its mover trait is disabled (`Drag.cs:49-50`), so an EMP'd unit mid-leg would sit off-map forever — **a suppression that replaces an unconditional sell has to guarantee the sell on every exit**, which is why the leg has three terminating conditions rather than one.
- **"Ejected crew should only defend themselves" needed NO new code — the stance was already right, and the thing making them fight was the bot.** AutoTarget gives every bot-owned actor `InitialEngagementStanceAI = Defensive` (`AutoTarget.cs:160`), and `Defensive` already means "return fire, never move toward a target" (`:617-619`). What put crew into fights was `PoiOffensiveBotModule` recruiting them and issuing `AttackMove`. **The second stance axis is easy to miss: `UnitStance` (HoldFire/Ambush/FireAtWill) and `EngagementStance` (HoldPosition/Defensive/Hunt) are independent, and `InitialStanceAI = FireAtWill` makes crew look aggressive when the axis that actually governs pursuit is already defensive.**
- **Ejected crew classify as `MainBattle`, so the AI puts pistol infantry in armoured pushes.** `UnitRoleResolver.Classify` reaches its "armed and mobile" fallback (`:379`) for them: a pistol is not indirect, not anti-air, not long-ranged, and their `Mobile.Speed` of 25 is far below `ReconSpeedFloor` 110. They appear in no `ExcludeFromSquadsTypes` list — not because they were considered and kept, but because nothing else ever looked at them. `CrewMemberInfo` is the robust classification hook: it is carried by exactly the ten `crew.*` actors and describes what the actor IS rather than how it is armed.
- **Crew really are worth more sold than fought, and this is NOT the emptied-supply-truck case.** `Valued.Cost` 100 (`crew.yaml:14-15`); their pistol magazine is worth 3 total (`SupplyValue: 1` × 3 batches), so `GetSellValue`'s ammo deduction is ~3% and the refund is ~100 × HP/MaxHP. The recent finding that an emptied supply truck sells for only its chassis does not transfer — that deduction is missing *supply* on a `SupplyProvider` host, and crew host none.
- **A per-module `HashSet` of "units I sent to evac" cannot be the general exclusion, but it also cannot be DELETED — the two guards cover different windows, and this is a latch-vs-observation split worth getting right.** `QueueActivity(false, act)` does **not** replace the current activity: `Cancel` only raises `IsCanceling`, and the new activity is appended *behind* the one still winding down (`Actor.cs:381-386` → `Activity.Queue`, `Activity.cs:212-217`). So for at least one tick after the order, `CurrentActivity` is still the OLD activity. **A head-only `is RotateToEdge` test therefore answers "not evacuating" about a unit that demonstrably is — and that one-tick window is exactly when a re-recruit cancels the evac.** Walking the queued chain closes it; the set remains useful for the module's own bookkeeping. `IsAlreadySeekingRearm` (`PoiOffensiveBotModule.cs:2388`) uses the head-only form safely only because it asks about activities that are already running.

  **Updated 2026-08-10 — that precondition was violated the first time anyone added a second caller, which is the argument for not having stated it as a precondition at all.** A unit trait began asking the same question *inside* the dispatch window (having just issued the errand itself), where the head is the cancelling `Move` and the resupply sits behind it. Infantry `Mobile.Speed: 25` ⇒ ~41 ticks to clear the current cell, against a 25-tick scan — so the head-only answer was wrong for longer than the interval between asks, producing a duplicate `Resupply` **and** a window in which `StarvingRecruitGate` reported the unit as available and the bot could destroy the errand. The helper is now `AmmoPool.IsSeekingRearm` and walks `NextActivity` to the end of the chain, so it is correct for both callers regardless of window. **General rule: a "is this actor already doing X" predicate should walk the queue unconditionally.** The head-only form is not an optimisation worth a precondition — the chain is two or three deep, and the precondition is invisible at the call site where it gets broken.
- **RESOLVED BY MEASUREMENT — `test-offense-ammo-guard` is RED at the BASE too, and the lesson is that a behavioural test can be invalidated by a fix landing underneath it.** Run at `bd3abacf`: FAIL `"EmptyTank died before verdict"`. Run at `02fe9eab`: FAIL `"advanced 24 cells east"`. Same cause, different seed. **The guard it tests works** — the run log shows `[exp-ooa] sweep ... evac=1 banked=2260` and `[exp-offense] reeval ... pool=6 free=0` on the same tick, i.e. the dry tank was swept to evac and excluded from the free pool. What broke is the test's PROXY for "was recruited": *moved more than 4 cells east*, plus a requirement that the tank still be alive at the verdict. Both assume a dry tank **stays parked** — true when the scenario was written, false since `8fb0c855` gave dry vehicles an evac disposition, because an evacuating tank drives to the edge and sells. **The general form: when you give a previously-inert failure state an ACTION, every test that asserted the inaction silently changes meaning — and it will keep reporting RED for the original defect long after that defect is fixed.** Worth checking the other `Skip*`/disposition scenarios for the same staleness.
- **Autotest triage can silently read the wrong game's log.** `run-test.sh:229-243` probes only `debug.log`, but OpenRA writes `debug.log.1` when a live instance holds the lock — so a test launched with the menu still open logs somewhere the harness never points you. Here `debug.log` was 13 minutes stale while the run's entire log, including the `[exp-ooa]` lines that explained the verdict, was in `debug.log.1`. This is the second time today the project has been slowed by unobservable bot behaviour; the earlier one was missing instrumentation, this one is instrumentation that exists and cannot be found.

## 2026-08-08 — `SectorPostureHold` has no terminating condition, and the shape of that failure is NOT settled: three candidate mechanisms fit the same live report, the code supports the one the first draft ruled out, and the fix bounds only two of them — plus the reason the existing commitment machinery produces no committed behaviour (it is unreachable, not mistuned)

Occasioned by the live 2026-08-08 report ("a group flanking to capture a side derrick is constantly ordered back, then forward again, and back, and seem stuck in a loop"). Static analysis + code read on `auto/posture-veto` (`3f78d7e5`, parent `e79ddd97`); **no game run — nothing below about observed dynamics is confirmed.** Written after an adversarial review refuted this entry's own first draft; the refutation is preserved because the mistake is the instructive part.

- **PREMISE CORRECTION, read this before re-picking from the old candidate list.** The 2026-08-03 diagnosis at the foot of this file records three unimplemented fix candidates; **(1) own-strength floor and (2) evaluate the axis's CONTACT sector instead of the deep target sector both SHIPPED the same day** in `82779a2e` (merge `7baa3885`), and `influence-stack.md:129` already documents them as current design. Only (3) "exempt the enemy-SR Pressure axis" was never built. Anyone reading that entry as a live to-do list will re-implement work already at HEAD.
- **THE DURABLE METHOD FINDING, and it cost a refuted draft to get: a control gate's failure SHAPE is a property of the whole order pipeline, not of the predicate.** `SectorPostureHold` is unclearable in all three stories below, but what an unclearable hold *looks like* depends entirely on where its fall-back order sends the units — which is decided two calls away in `ResolveMusterAnchor`. Reasoning from the predicate alone (as the first draft did) produced a confident, wrong dynamic. **Before describing an oscillation, trace the destination the gate actually orders and check whether the position it reads can move at all.**
- **CANDIDATE (A) — SILENT PERIOD-1 STALL. This is the one the shipping profile's code supports.** With `ForwardMusterEnabled: true` (`ai.yaml:614`) the posture fall-back destination is `ResolveMusterAnchor(axis) ?? stagingAnchor ?? rallyCell` (`PoiOffensiveBotModule.cs:2639`). `ResolveMusterAnchor` (`:2107-2155`) returns either a cell strictly CLOSER to the front — `ForwardStagingMath.StagingCell` only accepts a neighbour with strictly smaller frontier distance (`ForwardStagingMath.cs:120-124`) — or, on self-seed, the axis's own grid-cell centre; it returns `null` (the only path to the rearward anchor) **only when `axis.NearRally` is true**, which a deep-flank axis is not. So a held flank axis is never marched rearward. First hold issues an `AttackMove` to where it already stands; every subsequent hold emits **nothing**, because `OrderRetreat`'s `moved` guard sees an unchanged destination (`:3799-3803`). A silent freeze — no new order, no motion.
- **CANDIDATE (B) — PERIOD-2 OSCILLATION. Real mechanism, but it needs a rearward muster, so not this profile.** Where the muster does resolve rearward (`ForwardMusterEnabled` off, or a `NearRally` axis), holding moves the axis out of its sector ⇒ `sectorOwn` falls under `SectorPostureHoldOwnFloor` (3) ⇒ the floor **fails OPEN** (`FrontlineAllocationMath.cs:199-200`) ⇒ assault ⇒ units return ⇒ hold re-fires, one real order per flip since `axis.OrderedRetreat` alternates. **This was the first draft's confident diagnosis and it is refuted for `@experimental`.**
- **CANDIDATE (C) — SELECTION CHURN. Fits the live report at least as well as (B), and the fix is INERT against it.** The axis stalls per (A) ⇒ its POI churns out of the sticky top-k ⇒ the axis is DESTROYED (`ReleaseAxis "dropped"` `:1288`, or `"under-min"` at `MinAxisSize: 3` `:1428`; also `RetireAllAxes` `:3370`) ⇒ `StageFreePool` walks the orphaned units rearward ("ordered back") ⇒ a new axis re-forms for the same derrick and walks them forward again. Period is selection churn, not posture. **A destroyed axis takes `PostureHoldEvals` with it, so the budget is refunded every cycle and never accumulates.** Sharper still, and it is this entry's own commitment finding turned against its own fix: a posture-holding axis returns before `ApplyMissionCommitment`, so `Committed` is false, so `PartitionHeldAxes:1773` never protects it — **the axis spending the budget is precisely the one fully exposed to the reshuffle.** The bound needs ~`SectorPostureHoldMaxEvals` × `ReevaluateInterval` ≈ 300 uninterrupted ticks to bite; (C) is exactly what denies that.
- **THE SELF-REFERENCE IS REAL BUT WEAKER THAN FIRST CLAIMED — and it is STILL OPEN, not fixed here.** `SectorPostureHold` reads the sector the axis stands in, and own-strength there is a live count of our armed actors (`ControlField.cs:678-697`), so the gate can read its own consequence. But the honest bound is **not** "the axis IS `sectorOwn`": `FrontlineProfileMath.Accumulate` sums the **entire map-height column** (`ControlField.cs:315-341`; `grayBand` gates only the frontier-edge test, never the strength sum), counting own **and allied** actors including defensive structures — a sector is a full-height strip ~12 cells wide on river-zeta. The axis dominates `sectorOwn` only in a flank strip crossing none of our line, no garrison and no other axis. Near our own base it barely moves the number. (The "~1 per armed unit" scale claim does hold.) **The budget TRUNCATES this coupling; it does not remove it.** A non-self-referential predicate already exists in the file — `OwnAxisStrength(axis)` (`:3572`), which travels with the axis and is invariant to where it stands — but being position-invariant it would still need a budget to terminate. So the bound is necessary under either formulation and **the self-reference remains an open defect.**
- **WHY THE EXISTING COMMITMENT MACHINERY PRODUCES NO COMMITTED BEHAVIOUR: it is unreachable, not mistuned.** `ApplyMissionCommitment` is the ONLY writer of `axis.Committed` (`:2668-2678`) and `PartitionHeldAxes` opens with `if (!axis.Committed) continue;` (`:1773-1774`). All three fall-back gates — genuine retreat, damper, posture — RETURN *above* that snapshot, deliberately (a `Committed` axis is frozen out of its own release gate). The consequence is a **structural exclusion**: an axis that posture-holds is never stamped, so it is never held, so it re-enters the full reshuffle/re-task path every eval with **zero memory of what it decided last eval**. `MissionCommitmentWindowTicks` (400 ≈ 4 evals) and the `MissionIneffective` baseline are fine; they simply never apply to a held axis. **The commitment layer the user is asking for largely EXISTS — it is gated behind the very holds that need it.** This is the one claim here that is code-certain and shape-independent.
- **FIX (this branch): bound the hold rather than delete it** — `SectorPostureHoldMaxEvals` (`PoiOffensiveBotModule.cs:720`, `@experimental` `ai.yaml:604` = 3; **0 = unbounded = legacy**, so every other profile stays byte-identical), counter `Axis.PostureHoldEvals` (`:901`), pure helpers `FrontlineAllocationMath.PostureBudgetExhausted` / `StepPostureHold` (`:251/:262`). Mirrors `RetreatDamperMath.HoldBudgetExhausted`, added for the same class of failure. Bounds **(A)** and **(B)**; inert against **(C)**. The counter is deliberately MONOTONE over one **(target, uninterrupted axis existence)** — not "per mission" in any durable sense, since destruction refunds it — because a counter that decayed on the press half of a flipping verdict would refund the budget as fast as it is spent (pinned by `PostureBudgetIsMonotoneAcrossAFlippingVerdict`). Sole axis construction site `:1299`; explicit resets only on genuine mission breaks (retreat / lost units, `:3441/:3481`).
- **The re-route was audited and is the intended destination.** Suppressing an early-return hold hands the axis to the next gate: (a) the `OrderedRetreat` reset (`:2648` — correct, its last order really was a fall-back), and (b) `ApplyMissionCommitment`, stamping it `Committed`. That is the "decide, then stay in it" behaviour the stall prevents, and since a held axis skips `CommitAndOrder` entirely it is also what stops the hold re-firing. Gate ORDER is untouched, so the positional "posture never blocks a genuine retreat" guarantee is preserved.
- **SAFETY IS HANDED OFF, NOT PRESERVED UNCONDITIONALLY — the first draft's "safety is unaffected" was too strong.** `PartitionHeldAxes:1786-1787` does release a held axis on a losing force ratio, but **release ≠ retreat**: `CombatRetreatMath.ShouldReleaseHeld` (`:116-120`) uses a bare `LosingBeyond` with no sustain window, while `axis.Retreat` needs `RetreatSustainEvals: 2` consecutive losing evals. In that gap a losing axis re-enters `CommitAndOrder`, fails the retreat gate, and meets an exhausted posture gate that waves it through — **~2 evals (~200 ticks) pressing with no posture brake.** Bounded, and `NoReinforceLostFights` blocks top-up once Retreating. The real safety argument is different and stronger: exhausting posture **moves the decision to a better sensor** — the retreat gate compares the axis's own health-weighted build value against believed enemy COST within `ForceRatioRadiusCells` of its centroid (`:3572-3620`), a strictly more local and better-scaled read than posture's whole-column presence COUNTS.
- **NOT VERIFIED — needs a live run, and until one is done treat (A)/(B)/(C) as open.** Which mechanism the user actually saw; whether 3 is the right budget vs 1 or 6; whether committing a flank axis converts into captured side POIs or feeds it piecemeal into a defended derrick. Instrumentation shipped on this branch for exactly this: `[exp-posture] hold|override` at the gate (target + `TargetId`, centroid, `sectorOwn`/`sectorEnemy`/floor, `evals/max`) — **consecutive** hold lines with a static centroid and `sectorOwn` never crossing the floor ⇒ (A); **alternating** lines with a moving centroid crossing the floor ⇒ (B) — plus `[exp-offense] axis-new` and an extended `retire` line (both carrying `TargetId`, retire also `postureEvals`/`committed`), whose pairing exposes short axis lifetimes and budget refunds ⇒ (C). Without the create/retire pair a refund is invisible and (C) cannot be detected at all.

## 2026-08-08 — when two agents re-decide about each other on independent cadences, no damping fixes it; make ONE OF THEM STOP MOVING. A static destination converts a control problem into an arrival problem

Building drop-and-leave (`auto/supply-drop`) after three review rounds of damping the supply-truck evac loop failed to remove the visible dithering. The general lesson is about the *shape* of the fix, not about trucks.

- **Two independent defects turned out to be one shape, and one change dissolved both.** (a) The truck's follow/evac branch is a limit cycle by construction — the relief valve re-selects the same needy cluster the moment the truck cools, so approach → abort → approach never terminates (`WORKSPACE/recon/260808-truck-post-fix-behaviour.md` §1). (b) The pull side chases without bound — `SupplyHuntMath.WithinLeash` is applied inside `FindNearestUsableProvider` (`AutoSeekSupplies.cs:152`) and **nowhere else**, `CanServe` has no distance term at all (`:177-217`), and the approach rides `MoveWithinRange`, whose `ShouldStop` is only "am I at correct range" (`MoveWithinRange.cs:38-43`) while `MoveAdjacentTo.Tick` cancels and re-queues the inner move every time the target's cell changes (`MoveAdjacentTo.cs:95-100`). Infantry at speed 25 therefore chase a truck at 75 indefinitely, combat-inert. **Both are "the destination moves". Neither is fixable by tuning the mover.**
- **The diagnostic question that separates these from ordinary jitter: does the loop have a fixed point at all?** Damping (dwell, hysteresis, deadband, leash) bounds the AMPLITUDE of an excursion. It cannot create a fixed point where none exists. If the input that selects a destination is regenerated identically as soon as the action's effect decays, the loop is periodic by construction and every damping knob only changes its period. **Before adding a fourth damper, ask what state the system is supposed to settle INTO — if you cannot name it, you are damping something that has no equilibrium, and the fix is structural.**
- **The structural move is to make the destination an ACTOR-INDEPENDENT, STATIONARY thing.** Against a static target `MoveWithinRange.ShouldStop` becomes reachable, so the approach child terminates; a retry counter measured in approach attempts (`SeekSuppliesAndReturn.MaxApproachAttempts`) stops being consumed by aura entry/exit flips; and the selector cannot re-select a target that has moved, because it has not. That counter's failure mode is worth grepping for on its own: **a retry budget that counts ATTEMPTS rather than distance or time silently becomes a budget on target-oscillation frequency**, which is a quantity nobody chose.
- **Re-issuing an identical order every scan is only a bug when the destination is derived from the MOVER.** Every previous fix in this area added memory (`lastVia`, `evacState`, the leg model) to stop a receding target restarting a maneuver — all of them necessary because `DetourWaypoint` and `RetreatTarget` are both computed *from the truck's own position*. A destination derived from the map instead is idempotent under re-issue, so the new branch needs **no per-truck "already dispatched" memory at all**. That matters beyond tidiness: an "already dispatched" latch is precisely the defect species (a test that pins a branch while reading a term that cannot respond to the action it causes) that the three prior review rounds each found once. **The cheapest way to not add a fourth latch is to design the branch so it does not need memory.**
- **The remaining memory should stabilise the DESTINATION, not the DECISION.** `ForwardStagingMath.AnchorShifted` (Chebyshev hysteresis, `:168`) is applied to the anchor cell, outside the drop predicate; `SupplyDropMath.ShouldDrop` is memoryless and re-derived from scratch each scan. Hysteresis on a *position* cannot latch a branch — the worst it can do is keep aiming at a slightly stale cell.
- **Check that the action switches its own precondition off, and prefer that it do so through SEVERAL independent terms.** A drop empties the truck (fails the load gate), creates a cache at the anchor (fails the redundancy gate) and refills the soldiers who walk to it (fails the demand gate). Any one of the three suffices; three means a bug in one does not produce a repeat-fire loop. Pinned individually in `SupplyDropMathTest` rather than only as a combined "drops / does not drop" pair, so a regression names which term broke.
- **"EVERY TERM RESPONDS TO THE ACTION" IS NOT ENOUGH — ASK HOW LONG THE RESPONSE TAKES RELATIVE TO THE DECISION CADENCE.** Review found the first version dumped the entire fleet's supply at one cell. All three responsive terms respond to a *completed* drop, and completion takes a drive; the trucks are evaluated in one loop over unchanged world state, so on the first scan the conditions hold, every truck reads "nothing here yet", every truck passes, and all of them unload. The invariant survived literally and was useless. **The repaired form: a gate over a fleet must count what is COMMITTED, not what has LANDED** — sum the loads of units already dispatched to that target and treat them as though they had arrived. Derive that from the same map that suppresses duplicate re-issue, so there is one piece of state serving both jobs and no second one that can drift. **Coverage caveat, stated precisely because the first draft of this entry overclaimed it: the PREDICATE is pinned (committed supply counts toward redundancy; ground and in-flight sum rather than max), but the MECHANISM is not — the in-flight figure comes from a mid-scan write read back by later units in the same loop, and deleting that write leaves every pin green. Pure-math pins cannot reach an intra-loop write/read ordering; that needs a module-level harness.**
- **PURE-MATH PINS COVER PREDICATES, AND EVERY DEFECT ON THIS BRANCH WAS IN PLUMBING — so check each pin can actually go RED before counting it.** A test-quality pass found one of mine was a tautology over its own signature (`ShouldIssueDrop(false, …)` short-circuits on the first term), while its comment described a module block the pin never reached: deleting that block left it green. It was covering the single most valuable fix in the commit — a latch I had created and caught myself. **The repair is to extract the condition as a named predicate, route the production path through it, and pin BOTH polarities**, then verify by reverting the predicate and confirming the pins fail. **And where a fix genuinely cannot be pinned without mounting a world (a `Locomotor` passability test; a branch whose defect was that it did not EXIST), write the gap down explicitly rather than leaving a comment that implies coverage.** An admitted gap is fine; a false coverage claim is not, because the next audit counts it.
- **The invariant is about the DECISION; the defects were all in the ACTION.** "No latch, therefore safe" was correct and still missed four plumbing bugs, because a clean predicate says nothing about whether the pipeline can deliver, revoke, or avoid duplicating what it decided. This is the same shape as the lesson one branch earlier (the entry test was safe not because of its term but because a gate bounded it). **After proving a predicate sound, audit separately: can the module STOP what it started, can it avoid re-issuing what is already running, does the order actually reach the place the decision named, and what happens when N units run the decision simultaneously?**
- **A re-issue dedup keyed on "already dispatched" silently disables every retry the design was relying on.** Adding the dedup broke two self-correcting refusals (arrival on an occupied cell; a destination that became unreachable after issue), converting both from "retries next scan" into "parks forever" — a latch introduced by the fix for a stutter. **The repair is a responsive term, not a timeout: a unit that is IDLE while its errand's precondition still holds has finished that errand without effect, so the record is void.** `IsIdle` is the observable this codebase already uses for exactly that (`StepEvac`'s leg model uses it to notice a Move that never arrived). **Whenever you add suppression, enumerate what used to happen on the suppressed path and confirm each still happens.**

## 2026-08-08 — an unreachable destination does NOT fail loudly in OpenRA: the pathfinder returns no path, `Move` calls that "arrived", and any queued follow-on runs at the wrong cell

Found by adversarial review of the drop-and-leave errand, where the follow-on was "unload 750 supply". Worth recording on its own because the trap belongs to the engine, not to that feature, and any `MoveTo(cell)` → `CallFunc(do the thing)` chain inherits it.

- **The mechanism, end to end.** `PathFinder.FindPathToTargetCell` bails to `NoPath` up front when the target cell is terrain-inaccessible (`PathFinder.cs:95-99`: `CellAllowsMovement` plus a reachable movement cost). `Move.Tick` then sees `path.Count == 0`, sets `destination = mobile.ToCell` and completes within about two ticks (`Move.cs:174-178`). The activity therefore reports success at **the cell the unit was already standing on**, and the next activity in the chain runs there.
- **`MoveTo(cell, nearEnough)` does NOT protect against this, and it reads as though it does.** The tolerance is consumed only inside `PopPath`'s *actor-blocked* branch (`Move.cs:266-267`), which requires a path to exist in order to have a next cell. Actor-blocked destinations are genuinely handled — that branch is what the tolerance is for. **Terrain-impassable destinations produce no path at all and never reach the tolerance logic.** So a `nearEnough` argument buys nothing for the failure mode people reach for it to cover.
- **Therefore any queued action that assumes arrival needs its own arrival check** — compare the unit's cell against the ordered cell with the same tolerance the move was given, and refuse otherwise. Keep it as one number used by both (`DropsSupplyCacheInfo.DropAtToleranceCells`), because two constants that must agree will not.
- **Belief-field descents are a rich source of unreachable cells and must be passability-tested by their CALLER.** `ForwardStagingMath.StagingCell` guards grid bounds and believed danger and has no terrain awareness by design (it is pure math). Worse, `ControlField.GridCellToMapCell` returns ONE fixed cell of each coarse block — at `CellSize: 2`, the odd/odd cell — so the result is not merely coarse but *arbitrarily* placed within its block, and water, cliff or off-playable-bounds are all ordinary outcomes of a 20–40 step walk. `PoiOffensiveBotModule` already grant-tests its anchor for exactly this reason and does so on **both** of its return paths (fresh candidate and hysteresis-held), so downstream code need not know which path produced the cell — copy that discipline, including the held-anchor re-test.
- **Where the two lines of defence differ, and why you want both.** Testing the anchor before adopting it prevents dispatch; the arrival check catches a cell that became unreachable *after* issue. Only the second one holds when the world changes mid-errand.

## 2026-08-08 — dropping supply converts a mobile self-preserving asset into a static capturable one; that is the real cost of drop-and-leave, and it is not visible in the supply arithmetic

Recorded rather than fixed, because it is a disclosed design trade and the standoff distance is the lever that manages it.

- **The 750 stops being able to run away.** Inside a truck the supply can evacuate — that is literally what `ResupplyBehavior.Evacuate` and the danger-evac branch exist to do. Once it is a `SUPPLYCACHE` on the ground it has `Health: 5000`, `Armor: Light`, `Targetable: Ground, Structure`, **no `NoAutoTarget`** (so enemies engage it unprompted) and `ProximityCapturable` at `1c512` with `CaptorTypes: Player, Vehicle, Tank, Infantry` and **`Sticky: true`** (`misc.yaml:370-427`). One enemy scout walking into 1.5 cells takes it permanently.
- **So the standoff is not a comfort setting, it is the whole risk control**, and it trades against the pull side's reach: the crate must stay inside `AutoSeekSupplies`' 20-cell selection leash of the soldiers it was dropped for, or they cannot select it at all. Forward enough to be useful, far enough back not to be a gift — with no reclaim path, since crate → truck does not exist in this codebase.
- **The supply arithmetic hides this.** `SupplyCreditValue` is 750 on both the truck and the crate, so value looks conserved across the drop and only the 250 chassis appears to be at stake. That accounting is correct and incomplete: it prices the supply, not the *survivability* of the supply.

## 2026-08-08 — the WW3MOD supply truck ALREADY had a one-shot lifecycle; nobody had written it down, so design discussion silently assumed a shuttle

Establishing the post-drop lifecycle for drop-and-leave. The answer turned on facts about the economy that are not written anywhere in `DOCS/reference/`, and getting them wrong would have produced either a truck that sells itself at the map edge after every delivery or one that drives to a building that does not exist.

- **LOGISTICSCENTER is `Prerequisites: ~disabled` (`structures.yaml:364-367`) and appears only as a NEUTRAL CAPTURABLE** — two per map on River Zeta (`map.yaml:3994`, `:9088`), both `Owner: Neutral`. So the only actor in the game that can refill a truck is one the player may never own. Any design whose happy path is "drive back and restock" is describing the *uncommon* case. `SupplyDepotActorTypes: logisticscenter` and the capture lists (`ai.yaml:103`, `:122`) are the AI's only relationship with it.
- **An emptied AI truck is retired TODAY, and that is the status quo any new design has to be measured against.** `IsLowOnSupply` drops it from `SupplyFollowerBotModule`'s roster (`:909-915`), which idles it, which lets `DropsSupplyCache.ITick` fire `EvacuateOrRestock` (`:210-220`); TRUK ships `InitialResupplyBehaviorAI: Evacuate` (`vehicles.yaml:514-516`), so that branch goes straight to `RotateToEdge` and sells (`:244-248`). **The `Auto` branch that would try an LC first is unreachable for AI trucks.** So "the truck sells itself after delivering" is not a regression introduced by dropping — it is what already happens, wherever the supply went.
- **The economics are conserved, which is the fact that makes a one-shot model acceptable rather than wasteful.** TRUK is `Cost: 1000` / `SupplyCreditValue: 750`; SUPPLYCACHE is also `SupplyCreditValue: 750` and takes the truck's exact load via `SupplyInit` (`DropsSupplyCache.cs:118-123`). `CustomSellValue.GetSellValue` deducts `MissingSupplyValue` (`CustomSellValue.cs:48-51`), so a drained truck refunds 250 — the chassis. **Value moves from truck into crate; only the 250 chassis is at stake.** With `UnitLimits: truk: 4` and `GateResupplyOnAmmoNeed: true` (`ai-america.yaml:40`, `:104`) the builder calls in a replacement when ammo need exists, so the loop sustains itself.
- **Composing a multi-step errand as ONE ACTIVITY CHAIN rather than as a sequence of ORDERS is a correctness requirement for bots here, not a style preference.** `ModularBot` meters its own queue — `ordersToIssueThisTick = ceil(orders.Count / MinOrderQuotientPerTick)` (`ModularBot.cs:127`) — so two orders queued in the same module tick can be issued on *different world ticks*. Anything that inspects the actor in between (here `DropsSupplyCache.ITick`, which fires on **any** tick the truck is idle-and-empty) runs in the gap. `DeliverSupply` (`:153-175`) already demonstrates the correct idiom: `MoveTo` → `CallFunc` inside one `ResolveOrder`. **General rule: if a bot's step N+1 must not be preempted by a trait watching for the state step N creates, N and N+1 must be activities in one chain, not two orders.**
- **`DropsCrate.cs` is still a dead near-duplicate of `DropsSupplyCache` with no YAML reference, and `Button@DROP_ONE_SUPPLY` is a hidden remnant of a `SupplyPerUnit` model that no longer exists** (`CargoPanelLogic.cs:205-210` hides it unconditionally, with the reason in a comment). Both are traps for anyone estimating "does a partial drop already exist?" — it does not; `DropSupplyCacheHere` calls `SetSupply(0)` and is all-or-nothing.
- **THE SUPPLY-CACHE MERGE PATH IS DEAD CODE, and believing otherwise will mis-size whatever you build on top of it.** `DropSupplyCacheHere` merges into a cache already on the truck's cell (`:94-106`), and `CanDropCache` explicitly permits a cell occupied by another cache (`:81-83`) — but `SUPPLYCACHE` is a `Building` with `Footprint: x`, and `x` is `FootprintCellType.Occupied`, i.e. **a blocked cell** (`Building.cs:20-27`, and the `[Desc]` there says so outright). A ground vehicle can therefore never stand on a cache, so neither branch is reachable. **Consequence: nothing in the game coalesces crates, EVERY drop lands on a fresh cell, and any anti-stacking must be done by the thing choosing to drop.** I had reasoned the opposite way round and used "same-cell drops merge" to argue a redundancy gate only needed to cover near-misses; review caught it. **When a merge/coalesce path justifies a design, check that the two actors can physically co-occupy before relying on it.**
- **The entire supply path had ZERO logging until this change** — no `Log.Write`, `BotDebug`, `TextNotifications` or `Console.WriteLine` in `SupplyFollowerBotModule`, `SupplyLogisticsMath`, `AutoSeekSupplies`, `SupplyProvider` or `SeekSupplyProvider` — so a real 1.5 MB `debug.log` contained nothing about trucks and no setting could turn it on (`Debug.BotDebug` gates `AIUtils.BotDebug`, which routes to chat, and nothing here called it). Two days of reasoning about this subsystem had no way to check its premise. **The split that keeps volume honest: EDGES unconditional (adopt/release, evac enter/exit, drop issued — rare, and exactly the transitions that were unrecoverable), LEVELS behind a flag (per-scan summaries, per-cluster readings, the reason a drop was declined).** Note also that `launch-game.cmd:46` and `ww3-dev.ps1:148` both point log cleanup at `Documents\OpenRA\Logs`, which does not exist — the real path is `%APPDATA%\OpenRA\Logs` (`Platform.cs:174`), so that cleanup step is a silent no-op.

## 2026-08-07 — a `GroundDanger` threshold does not select a DANGER LEVEL, it selects a GEOMETRIC CONTOUR of a believed weapon envelope — so every tuned threshold on that field means something other than what it looks like

Second finding of the day about the same field, and independent of the aliasing one below: that entry says single-cell reads are *noisy*, this one says the *scale* is not what threshold-tuners assume. Both make the same tuning untrustworthy, for different reasons.

- **The field's dynamic range per cell is enormous, so a threshold is a boundary in SPACE, not a level of concern.** A contact stamps `kernel.Intensity * (r - d + 1) / (r + 1)` (`DangerFieldLayer.cs:366`), i.e. a linear ramp from the weapon's reach down to zero. Calibrating off real weapon data: an ordinary rifle (`5.56mm.AR`, `weapons-ballistics.yaml:111-117`) yields intensity ≈1333 with a per-cell falloff of ~78 at minimum durability weight and ~235 at realistic weight; `73mm_BMP` runs into the **millions**. Any cell inside any fresh contact's radius therefore reads far above a threshold of 60 — typically by orders of magnitude. **A threshold of 60 and a threshold of 45 select very nearly the same set of cells**: both are "just inside the believed weapon envelope", differing by well under one cell of radius.
- **Consequence: hysteresis on this field is decorative unless it is comparable to the per-cell step.** `EvacReleaseHysteresis: 15` reads like a textbook Schmitt deadband, and the design narrative leaned on the resulting 45/60 band — but 15 units is **spatially sub-cell**, so a moving truck crosses the entire band in one step and never dwells inside it. Nothing about the oscillation is damped by it. What actually stabilises that loop is purely temporal: the dwell (branch cannot be re-decided mid-leg) and the leg model (retreat not re-issued until driven).
