The knob is kept because it picks *which contour* counts as the edge — a delivery-margin lever — but the prose that called it a deadband was overclaiming and has been corrected in `SupplyLogisticsMath`, `SupplyFollowerBotModule` and `ai.yaml`. **General rule: before writing hysteresis on a believed-field threshold, compute the field's typical step between adjacent cells. If the band is narrower than one step, it is a comment, not a mechanism.**
- **The same suspicion applies to every other tuned threshold on this field, and should be recorded as such.** `PoiOffensiveBotModule`'s `BelievedDangerMildThreshold` 40 / `HostileThreshold` 120 (`:188/:192`) and `GroundDangerNav`'s `GroundDangerSafeThreshold` 15 are already flagged in the aliasing entry below as unaudited *single-cell* reads; add that their **numeric values** are suspect for this second, independent reason. `influence-stack.md:86` already reaches the right conclusion for the routing case — "correctness against the ambient baseline is the strict-improvement rule, not the threshold" — and that reasoning generalises: on this field, **relative comparisons are meaningful and absolute thresholds mostly are not.**
- **Where that leaves the truck's approach contract (bounded, but NOT confirmed — this is the part a live run would settle).** Because the abort fires at roughly the enemy's weapon radius, the gap the truck must still close to deliver is `g ≈ (enemy weapon radius) − (enemy-to-our-units distance)`, and delivery needs overshoot `≥ g − 5` (the truck's 5-cell aura). That overshoot is supplied by the 150-tick scan interval landing the abort 0–11 cells past the contour. Rifle-vs-rifle at ~10 cells: delivers in most scan phases. Tank/ATGM at ~15: roughly two phases in three. A long-range weapon in a close assault: never. **So the "approach the least-bad cluster and abort on your own reading" contract is sound in principle, but its margin is set by a quantity this module neither controls nor measures — and what rescues the common case is the coarseness of the scan interval, which is accidental rather than designed.** If "trucks still don't resupply" is reported later, look here first; it is not a regression from the oscillation work.

## 2026-08-07 — the danger field is ALIASED against the control grid: any consumer that THRESHOLDS `GroundDanger` at a single cell is deciding on lattice parity, not on danger

Found while gating supply-truck cluster selection on believed danger (`auto/supply-dwell`). This is **not specific to that module** — it applies to every binary gate anywhere in the stack, so it is recorded on its own.

- **Three of every four map cells carry ZERO territory baseline.** `DangerFieldLayer.StampBaseline` (`:434-462`) writes its contribution to `controlField.GridCellToMapCell(cx + dgx, cy + dgy)`, and that mapping is `(gx * CellSize + CellSize / 2, gy * CellSize + CellSize / 2)` (`ControlField.cs:865-866`). At the shipping `CellSize = 2` (`:389`) the stamped set is exactly the map cells with **both coordinates odd**. The dense contact kernel (`:362-372`) is stamped per map cell and is unaffected; it is the *baseline* that is sampled on a lattice.
- **The aliased component is large, and it is documented as large.** `BaselineIntensity` is only 5 (`:217`), but it stamps **additively from every frontier cell**, and `DOCS/reference/influence-stack.md:46` states outright that "a dense sector's baseline **stacks** and can exceed 40 easily — do not assume it is a small constant". So near a contested frontier a single-cell `GroundDanger` read can differ by **more than 40 between two adjacent cells** purely on parity. Any hysteresis band narrower than that (mine was 15) is smaller than the quantisation noise it is trying to reject: **the loop is closed but not damped.**
- **This bites GATES far harder than it bites GRADIENTS.** A consumer that takes an argmin/argmax over a neighbourhood (`GroundDangerNav.DetourWaypoint`, `ForwardStagingMath` steepest descent) samples many cells and the lattice averages out. A consumer that asks `danger(cell) >= threshold` about ONE cell gets a coin flip on that cell's parity. `influence-stack.md:86` already says the right thing for the gradient case — "correctness against the ambient baseline is the strict-improvement rule, not the threshold" — and the threshold consumers are exactly the ones that rule does not cover.
- **De-alias by taking the MAX over the cell and its grid-centre representative** (`controlField.GridCellToMapCell(controlField.MapCellToGridCell(cell))`). Two reads, no `CellSize` assumption: the representative carries the stamped baseline for the whole grid cell, the cell itself carries its own dense kernel value. **MAX, and the reason differs per alternative** — `min` reports the unstamped member, i.e. under-reports danger, which is the wrong direction for a safety gate; `mean` does **not** reintroduce parity (a mean over a fixed block is uniform within it — an earlier version of this entry said otherwise and was wrong), its defect is on the *other* term, diluting the densely-stamped contact kernel across four cells and roughly quartering the local peak the gate exists to notice. Max is the only one of the three that preserves both terms. Implemented as `SupplyFollowerBotModule.GroundDangerAt`; **every binary gate in a module must route through one such helper**, or the entry and exit tests disagree about what the same cell reads and the band between them stops being real.
- **De-aliasing RAISES readings, which can quietly promote a "fallback" branch into the main path — check what your gate's pass rate becomes.** `max` lifts every cell to at least its control-block baseline, and that baseline alone stacks past 40 against a gate at 45. So near any contested frontier the "comfortable" set is often empty *before a single weapon is counted*, and the relief-valve branch is not a rare corner case — it is the ordinary in-contact path, firing precisely when a cluster is in a firefight, i.e. when resupply matters most. That matters because a branch you believe is rare gets written with weaker invariants: here the valve returns an *ungated* danger reading, and feeding it to a gate that assumed gated input relatched the whole bug (below). **After changing how a field is sampled, re-derive which side of each threshold the typical reading now falls on.**
- **Worth auditing:** any other `GroundDanger(...) >= someThreshold` / `<= someThreshold` on a single cell. `PoiOffensiveBotModule`'s `BelievedDangerFactor` buckets (mild 40 / hostile 120, `:188/:192`) and `GroundDangerNav`'s `GroundDangerSafeThreshold` early-out are single-cell threshold reads on the same field; they were not touched here and their exposure has not been measured. **Two independent reasons to distrust them**, and an audit should treat them separately: the sampling is parity-noisy (this entry), and the threshold VALUES do not mean what they look like because the field's per-cell step near a contact dwarfs them (the contour entry above).

## 2026-08-07 — supply-truck oscillation (`auto/supply-dwell`): a damper alone would NOT have fixed it — the loop was open, because the input that drove the decision could not respond to the action it caused

Fixing the "trucks drive part-way forward, get ordered back to the SR, repeat" symptom. The brief framed it as a missing dwell (as in `31409790`'s infantry `RetreatDamperMath`). A dwell was necessary but is **not sufficient**, and the reason generalises to every believed-field consumer.

- **A hysteresis/dwell can only damp a loop that is CLOSED. `ShouldEvacuate` was open.** The decision was `dangerAtTruck >= T || dangerAtCluster >= T` (`SupplyLogisticsMath.cs:111-114` pre-fix), and the action it triggers — retreat 12 cells toward the SR — moves the truck, so it changes `dangerAtTruck` and **cannot change `dangerAtCluster` at all**. While the assigned cluster read hot the second disjunct held the decision true forever, so the truck re-took the retreat every scan until the geometry clamped it at the SR. Adding dwell to that only makes the retreat *periodic* instead of *continuous*; the fixed point still does not exist. **General rule: before adding hysteresis to a believed-field decision, check that every term in it is downstream of the action. A term that is not is a constant with respect to the controller, and no amount of damping makes it settle.**
- **Stating that rule is not the same as applying it — the first version of this fix reintroduced the identical bug one threshold lower, and review caught it.** Having correctly identified the non-responsive term, I gated *selection* at the entry threshold (60) but left the *release* test calling a shared helper that still ORed the destination reading, at `ReleaseLevel` (45). Every destination the gate admitted — the whole `[45, 59]` band — therefore made the release permanently true regardless of where the truck drove: the reported symptom at full amplitude, with a period of minutes instead of seconds. **Two lessons, both cheap to apply next time.** (1) **An entry test and a release test must not share a helper**, because the release is the one that has to be able to become false, so it has a strictly narrower set of admissible terms; a shared helper silently grants the release whatever the entry is allowed to read. (2) **Where a gate admits and a threshold releases, the two numbers must be the same** — any gap between "I will go here" and "I will leave here" is not a deadband, it is a latch the size of the gap. Both are now stated as a `RESPONSIVE-TERMS INVARIANT` at `EvacuateWithDwell` and pinned.
- **The invariant's real form is broader than "the release test", and I had to be told twice to get there.** Having fixed the release, I added a relief valve whose whole job is to hand back an *ungated* destination reading — and fed it straight into the ENTRY test, which short-circuits ahead of both the dwell and the release. Entry then pinned the branch true on every scan regardless of the truck's position: leg to the SR, drift out of follow range, release, re-select the same cluster through the valve, re-enter. Parked at the SR resupplying nobody — the exact starvation the valve existed to prevent, reached by a longer road. The general statement, which does cover both mistakes: **any test that can pin a branch true may read only responsive terms, UNLESS its non-responsive terms are bounded by a gate applied in the same scan.** The entry test was never safe because of the term; it was safe because the selection gate bounded it. **So when you add a path that bypasses a gate, enumerate every consumer that was relying on that gate for its bounds** — the bypass is invisible at the consumer, which is why the fix belongs in a named function (`DestinationDanger`) rather than an inline test at the call site.
- **A named enforcement point is not enforcement — get the FLAG POLARITY right and the invariant becomes structural.** The helper still takes the trust decision as a caller-supplied bool, so it documents the invariant without imposing it. What imposes it is which way the flag defaults: tracking `Relieved` (default false = "trusted") means any future selection path that forgets to set it silently restores the latch, whereas tracking `Gated` (default false = "not known safe") means forgetting it costs a little sensitivity and nothing else. **When a boolean decides whether downstream code may trust a value, name it for the property that had to be ESTABLISHED, not the exception that has to be REMEMBERED — and set it at the point the property is established, so it can never claim more than was actually checked.** Cheap, and it converts "we documented the rule" into "forgetting the rule fails safe".
- **The pins passed the whole time, because every release assertion used `dangerAtCluster: 0`.** A test suite that only exercises the zero value of the term whose *non-zero* behaviour is the bug proves nothing about it. When pinning a predicate written to reject a specific defect, at least one assertion must carry a non-zero value for **each** argument the defect involves — the FIX-1 pin is `Evac(wasEvacuating: true, hold: 0, dangerAtTruck: 0, dangerAtDestination: 50) == false`, and it was verified to FAIL against the reverted line before being kept.
- **A dwell counter armed on the entry edge does not bound the ACTION, only the DECISION — those need separate mechanisms.** `StepEvacDwell` re-arms only on false→true, which is correct for keeping the counter terminating. But it means that from the scan after the dwell expires, `hold` is 0 on every subsequent scan, so a re-issue gated on `hold <= 0` fires *every* scan. Since `RetreatTarget` steps from the truck's own moving position, that is a receding target: at TRUK's speed (~11 cells covered per 150-tick scan against a 12-cell leg) the truck never arrives and simply walks to the SR — the original failure, reached by a different route. Bounding the distance needs its own mechanism: issue one leg, then leave it alone until it has actually been driven (arrival within `RepathThresholdCells`, **or `IsIdle`** — without the idle clause a truck whose Move failed on a blocked cell is stranded in the danger it was trying to leave).
- **Selection merit and rejection criteria were positively correlated, and that is a design smell worth grepping for.** `OrderByDescending(c => c.AmmoNeed)` picks the cluster that has been fighting; "has been fighting" is the definition of high believed danger; high believed danger is exactly what the evac rule refuses to approach. The module systematically chose the target it was about to refuse. **Where a module has both a "pick the best X" and a "but not if X is too Y" rule, check whether the merit and the veto are computed from correlated quantities — if they are, the module will thrash regardless of how well either rule is written.** The structural cure is to apply the veto as a FILTER ON THE CANDIDATE SET, not as a post-selection rejection: a filter makes the two consistent by construction, whereas softening the merit (need weighted against danger) only lowers the frequency at which a large enough need still selects a vetoed target.
- **A veto must be gated on the cell that will actually be ORDERED, not on the one the candidate is named by.** I first gated on `UnitCluster.CenterCell` while the truck is sent to `FindSafeFollowPosition(cluster)` — and that function takes the argmax of `-ThreatMapManager.GetThreat` over a ±3 box, where `GetThreat` is `enemyValue - friendlyValue` (`ThreatMapManager.cs:196/:228`). Minimising it therefore **deliberately walks toward the friendliest-dense cell, i.e. toward the contact line**, up to ~4 cells off the centroid. A centroid reading safe can hand out a follow cell that is not, so the veto and the destination were computed from two different quantities and the gate could be satisfied by a cell the truck never goes to. **Whenever a "is this target safe?" test and a "where do I actually send the unit?" computation are separate functions, verify they agree on the cell — a safety gate on a proxy position is not a safety gate.**
- **A hard veto needs a relief valve, and "the set is empty" is not a safe default.** Vetoing every hot cluster can empty the candidate set, and the fall-through is a bare `continue` — the truck simply parks. Worse, the idle-truck hunt that would otherwise catch it is behind an explicit `@experimental` BotType gate, so on `@stable` the veto silently converts "resupply carefully" into "no resupply at all". Compounding it, `FindUnitClusters` applies **no need gate whatsoever**, so full-ammo rear clusters are candidates with `AmmoNeed` 0 — veto the needy one and need-descending ordering confidently hands the truck a cluster that needs nothing. The shape that works: require real need to be a candidate at all, prefer the safe ones, and when none are safe fall back to the **least dangerous cluster that still has need**, leaving the (undamped) entry test to stop the truck if it genuinely becomes too hot — and mark that fallback so no downstream gate mistakes its reading for a gated one. **When adding a veto to a selector, always ask what happens when it matches everything, and check whether the fallback path is gated to a narrower audience than the veto is.** The resulting contract is worth naming rather than leaving implicit, because it is what the two branches together mean: **gate to comfortable cells where any exist, otherwise approach the least-bad one and abort on your own reading.** Capping the fallback at the veto threshold instead is not a simplification — it just restores park-and-starve for the exact regime the fallback exists for.
- **Anti-oscillation dwell must be ASYMMETRIC or it becomes a safety defect.** Freezing the branch decision in both directions would delay a genuine withdrawal by up to the dwell. `EvacuateWithDwell` tests the entry threshold FIRST and returns true regardless of the counter, so only the RETURN to following is damped — the same guard `RetreatDamperMath.ShouldHold` states as its load-bearing property. Pinned as a test, not just a comment, because it is the property most likely to be silently lost in a later refactor.
- **A dwell counter armed on the ENTRY EDGE terminates; one armed on every in-state eval does not.** `StepEvacDwell(hold, startedEvacuating, dwell)` re-arms only on the false→true transition. Re-arming on every evacuating scan would make the counter unable to expire while the truck stayed hot, converting the dwell into a latch. This is the mirror image of the `StepFillHold` lesson (2026-08-05 entry): there the counter had to PERSIST across a release to stop refunding its budget; here it must NOT persist across the in-state evals to stop becoming permanent. Both bugs are "the counter is stepped on the wrong event", in opposite directions — when adding a hold counter, write down explicitly which EDGE arms it and which EVENT clears it.
- **The re-issue suppression is a second, separate mechanism from the decision dwell — do not collapse them.** The evac Move's destination is `RetreatTarget(truck.CenterPosition, sr, 12c)`, computed from the truck's OWN moving position, so re-issuing it each scan recomputes a shorter leg from a nearer place and restarts the maneuver before it completes (the receding-target restart `057ab755` cured for the Stage-E detour branch). But the FOLLOW branch's re-issue is *intended* — it tracks a moving cluster. So suppression is applied per-branch (`heldBefore <= 0` gates only the evac Move) while the dwell gates the branch choice. A single "don't re-issue anything" deadband would have broken cluster following.
- **`InfluenceStack.Participates` is no longer an `@experimental` narrowing, and a lot of in-tree prose still says it is.** Since `b8d2e601` (2026-08-02, user-authorised `@stable` parity promotion) it returns true for `StableBotType` too (`InfluenceStack.cs:47-48`). Every flag gated on `participates` ALONE therefore ships to both fog-respecting bots. Post-promotion authors knew — `IdleTruckHunt` and `CommitGarrisonedUnits` carry explicit `player.BotType == ExperimentalBotType` gates — but flags written BEFORE 2026-08-02 kept comments claiming `@stable` byte-identity that quietly stopped being true (`SectorSpread`/`SmallSquadCoverage`/`DangerEvac`, all from `be1d1615` on 2026-07-30). **When reading a "double-gated on Participates ⇒ every other profile byte-identical" comment, check the flag's commit date against 2026-08-02 before believing it.** Corrected in `SupplyFollowerBotModule`, `DangerFieldLayer.cs:300`, and the `ai.yaml` `@supply` block; the `[low, doc-in-code]` bug entry of 2026-08-04 lists three more of the same family in `ai.yaml`.
- **`SupplyProvider`'s self-restock is INERT for every AI truck, and two in-tree comments were built on the assumption it was live.** All three `TryRestock` call sites are gated on `ShouldSelfRestock()`, which returns false under `ResupplyBehavior.Evacuate` (`SupplyProvider.cs:330-338`), and TRUK ships `InitialResupplyBehaviorAI: Evacuate` (`vehicles.yaml:514-516`). So `SupplyFollowerBotModule`'s "don't cancel the restock" justification (the premise of `35804ddc`) protects a path that does not exist for the AI; what actually collects a released truck is `DropsSupplyCache`, which drives it to the MAP EDGE and sells it. Similarly `SupplyProvider.SetTarget`'s `MoveTo` (`:516-524`) is unreachable outside Hunt stance — its gate is `!InAuraRange(...)` but the non-Hunt target search only enumerates actors already inside the aura, so the gate is false by construction. **Neither is a truck-movement source; a truck's only movers are `SupplyFollowerBotModule` and `DropsSupplyCache`.**
- **`GarrisonBotModule`'s eligibility fallback is not infantry-only, and the module issued orders it could prove were no-ops.** With `GarrisonActorTypes` unset (the live mod state) `IsGarrisonEligible` falls back to `HasTraitInfo<PassengerInfo>()`, and `^WheeledVehicle` grants `Passenger` (`vehicles.yaml:116-123`) — so supply trucks qualify. The `EnterTransport` order is then discarded by `Passenger.ResolveOrder`'s `IsCorrectCargoType` (garrison buildings take `Types: Infantry`, the truck is `CargoType: Vehicle`), but the `blackboard.ClaimUnit` had already been taken and the module has **no `ReleaseUnit` call anywhere**, so the truck was removed from every other module's pool permanently. **Narrower in practice than the static read suggests: the candidate filter requires `a.IsIdle`, so only a PARKED truck is grabbable — an actively-following one is immune.** Fixed by matching cargo types at the pairing site (the same test the order itself runs) plus a real claim/release lifecycle. General shape: **if a module claims a unit at order-issue time, the claim's release path must be written in the same commit — grep for `ClaimUnit` without a matching `ReleaseUnit` in the same file.**

## 2026-08-05 — SR flow shape (`auto/spawn-flow`): the fresh-spawn "assembly wait" was ONE gate, and the other four things that look like it are different disciplines

> **[rejected: branch narrative for a default-off `@experimental` lever (`ImmediateReinforcementCommit`) tied to the spawn-flow fork record — per-branch enablement state, inert-knob bookkeeping (`MinAdvanceStrength` / `MaxAdvanceHoldEvals` short-circuited) and dead telemetry (`axis.FillHoldEvals`) are not durable reference. Its two general halves were banked from the Wave B entry below instead: *a hold gated on a rally-proximity predicate is a spawn-flow gate whatever its design intent* → influence-stack.md §Frontline reachability & staging (P3 damper bullet), and *the hold gates are early returns, so removing one is a RE-ROUTE, not a subtraction — audit the successor gate* → same doc, P5 gate-ordering bullet.]** (curation 2026-08-06).

Implementing the user's SR-flow-shape pick ("advance immediately, singly — zero assembly anywhere") meant first separating the wait from four look-alikes. Findings:

- **Only ONE gate in the offensive module makes a unit that ALREADY HAS a demand point defer going to it in order to gather: `RetreatDamperMath.ShouldHold` arm (b), the fill-completion massing hold** (`PoiOffensiveBotModule.cs` `DamperShouldHold` → the `damperHold` consumer at the top of `CommitAndOrder`). It is reachable only with `axis.NearRally` true, i.e. only for an axis whose CENTROID is inside `RetreatSafeDistanceCells` (10) of the SR — PREDOMINANTLY, but not exclusively, a newly-formed axis whose units are still walking in from the beachhead. `NearRally` is a bare position test (Chebyshev), so an ESTABLISHED axis inside that bubble also trips arm (b) whenever it reads `FillIncomplete`: its `AllocatedSize` was raised this eval, casualties dropped it below allocation, or its POI simply sits inside the SR bubble. Fresh spawns are the dominant population, not the gate's definition. That, plus its `MaxAdvanceHoldEvals` cap, is what the fork record meant by "forward-assemble with a **capped wait** at the forward muster". Everything else that reads like assembly is not: **arm (a)** is a post-retreat dwell (a unit set that already fought), **`SectorPostureHold`** is a posture decision at the line, **`StageFreePool`** is the disposition of units the allocator funded NO axis for (they hold no demand point to be advanced at — bypassing it would strand them idling on the SR road, which is the original pooling symptom, not a cure), and **transport-fill waits** are loading. All four are left live; the muster machinery (`ResolveMusterAnchor` / `ResolveStagingAnchor`) still serves all of them.
- **A hold that is gated on a *distance-to-rally* predicate is a spawn-flow gate whether or not it was designed as one.** Arm (b) shipped as an anti-oscillation lever (stop 2-3-unit packets trickling forward), and its reshape history is all about *termination* (Wave B: fill-completion + a capped, per-episode-sticky budget). But `NearRally` is a spawn-proximity test, so in practice the lever fires overwhelmingly on reinforcements forming up at the beachhead — enough that it is a spawn-flow gate for design purposes, while never being *only* that (see the bullet above for the established-axis cases it also catches). When auditing "does anything delay fresh spawns", grep the hold predicates for rally/SR-proximity terms rather than for intent words like *muster* or *staging* — the naming does not track the consumer.
- **`PoiOffensiveBotModule` needs no `BotType` confinement for a new lever, unlike the shared enable-ai-any modules.** It is instantiated as per-profile twins (`@experimental` / `@stable`, each behind its own `RequiresCondition`), so a C#-default-false field the `@stable` block omits is already byte-identical — no `CommitOnOrderMath.ShouldCommitShared` / `SupplyTruckHuntMath.ShouldHunt`-style `isExperimentalBot` conjunct is required here. (Contrast the 2026-08-04 entry below: `InfluenceStack.Participates` stopped discriminating profiles, which is what forces the explicit gate on SHARED instances. The distinction is instance topology, not the flag.)
- **Turning a lever off by making its gate unreachable leaves live YAML knobs that no longer do anything — say so at the knob.** With `ImmediateReinforcementCommit: true`, `MinAdvanceStrength` and `MaxAdvanceHoldEvals` are parsed as always but their SOLE READER is skipped: `DamperShouldHold` is `!SuppressMassingHold(...) && ShouldHold(...)`, so when suppression fires the `&&` SHORT-CIRCUITS and `ShouldHold` is never called — its arguments (including the `OwnAxisStrength(axis)` walk) are never even evaluated. They are inert because nothing reads them, not because a reader ignores them; the distinction matters because the short-circuit also means the knobs cost nothing at runtime while the flag is on. Deliberately kept as the revert path (dropping the one enable line restores the forward-assemble shape), with the inertness stated in both the `[Desc]` and the YAML comment so a later tuner doesn't sweep a dead knob.
- **`axis.FillHoldEvals` is DEAD TELEMETRY while the flag is on — do not tune `MaxAdvanceHoldEvals` from it.** The counter is still stepped every eval by `StepFillHold`, but with arm (b) suppressed it can only ever be incremented by a dwell hold, and its sole reader (`HoldBudgetExhausted`, inside the suppressed arm) is unreachable. So a log or sweep that reads it under `@experimental` is measuring dwells, not massing.
- **Removing a HOLD is a re-route, not a subtraction — enumerate what the axis newly reaches.** The damper hold is an early `return` in `CommitAndOrder`, so suppressing arm (b) lets a still-filling near-rally axis fall through to `SectorPostureHold` and `ApplyMissionCommitment`, both previously unreachable while filling. The second one bites: the axis can be stamped `Committed` with `CommitStrength` snapshotted at PARTIAL fill, and `PartitionHeldAxes` then freezes it out of re-sizing/top-up for `MissionCommitmentWindowTicks` (400 = 4 evals at `ReevaluateInterval` 100) with the `MissionIneffective` abort baseline anchored to that partial strength. Doctrine-consistent here (committing at partial fill IS "advance immediately"), and not stranding (held axes are excluded before `BuildFreePool`, so the un-handed units stay in the pool for `StageFreePool`) — but it is a state-space widening that no "byte-identical" phrasing may cover. General rule for gate work in this module: the hold gates are early returns, so deleting one always hands the axis to the NEXT gate; audit that successor before claiming the change only removes behaviour.
- **Pre-existing, noted while reading (not touched):** the grouped `AttackMove` an axis issues is re-issued for the WHOLE axis on every unit-set change, so each new recruit micro-repaths the units already en route. That frequency predates this wave — immediate commit raises how often the set changes, it does not introduce the re-issue.

## 2026-08-05 — "the @experimental bot builds ZERO riflemen" (`auto/composition-baseline`): **the army bar is not a census — it is an `AddToArmyValue` filter**, and the whole reported symptom was a display artifact sitting on top of two real purchase-lane leaks

> **[promoted (partial): → architecture.md new §`UpdatesPlayerStatistics.AddToArmyValue` is a win-rule input (default FALSE `PlayerStatistics.cs:258`; alive tally gated at `:342-347`; feeds `WeightedComponentMatchScorer.cs:84-91` → `TimeOrSrCaptureWinRule:88/:59`; nothing in the SIM branches on it — both victory traits sort on `Experience`, `ConquestVictoryConditions.cs:93` / `StrategicVictoryConditions.cs:136`) and → new §Call-in bookkeeping (`UnitLimits` counts WORLD ACTORS so it lags the whole map-edge walk-in; the affordability-filtered argmax degenerating into a cheapest-type pump). Current YAML state verified: the five templates now carry the flag (`infantry.yaml:1179/1298/1364/1436/2207`), `^Soldier:175` deliberately does not. **TWO CLAIMS IN THIS ENTRY ARE WRONG.** (1) “`AdaptiveProductionBotModule` rides the same FIFO lane for its counter-buys (`at`/`aa`/`e3`/`ar`, `ai.yaml:694-696`)” — `ai.yaml:694-696` is `ScoutBotModule@america`; the Adaptive instances are `:768`/`:812` (@experimental) and `:1563`/`:1582` (@stable), and their pools are faction-suffixed (`e3.america, ar.america, humvee`). The routing claim itself holds. (2) “`IsCompositionCandidateEligible` **ends** with an affordability test” — affordability is at `:1010`; the method ends `return HasAdequateAirUnitReloadBuildings(actorInfo);` at `:1013`. Also SUPERSEDED: “the external-request FIFO buys with NO caps at all” — a composition ceiling is now applied at the call site (`RequestIsOverCompositionCeiling`, `:400`). NOT banked: the arithmetic-refutation narrative, the provable-no-op argmax proof, and the LocalRandom-divergence note (all correct, all branch-local).]** (curation 2026-08-06).

Reported live-play observation: a long @experimental game showing 23 mortars, 3 snipers, 11 AT, 19 AA, 11 engineers, 12 medics and **zero** riflemen / LMG / grenadiers.

- **The observer army bar can only ever show types with `UpdatesPlayerStatistics.AddToArmyValue: true`, and that flag defaults to FALSE.** `ObserverArmyIconsWidget.cs:95` renders `playerStatistics.Units.Values`; the per-type alive tally `playerStats.Units[actorName].Count++` is incremented **only inside `if (includedInArmyValue)`** (`PlayerStatistics.cs:341-346`, mirrored on the decrement at `:298-302`), and `includedInArmyValue = info.AddToArmyValue` with `AddToArmyValue = false` as the default (`:258`). So a type without the flag reads count 0 in the bar **no matter how many are alive**. In `infantry.yaml`, `^Soldier:175` declares a bare `UpdatesPlayerStatistics:` (⇒ false) and only `^MT`/`^SN`/`^AT`/`^AA`/`^E6`/`^E4`/`^SF`/`^MEDI` set it true. `^E3` (Rifleman), `^AR` (LMG), `^E2` (Grenadier), `^TL` (Team Leader) and `^TECN` did not. **The six types the user saw are exactly `{AddToArmyValue: true} ∩ {the bot's UnitsToBuild roster}`** — E4/SF carry the flag but aren't on the roster, so they're absent too. The three "missing" types are exactly the ones lacking the flag. 6/6 and 3/3: the bar was reporting its own filter, not the army. Fixed by flagging the four line-infantry templates (plus `^TECN`, which carried no `UpdatesPlayerStatistics` node at all — its chain is `^ArmedCivilian`/`^CivInfantry`/`^Infantry`, none of which declare one, so technicians counted toward neither ArmyValue nor AssetsValue nor the alive tally nor `UnitTypeStats`).
- **`AddToArmyValue` is a WIN-RULE INPUT, not telemetry — the first version of this entry got that wrong and it is the more important half.** It feeds `PlayerStatistics.ArmyValue`, which `WeightedComponentScoring.Compute` folds into `snapshot.Total` (`WeightedComponentMatchScorer.cs:84-91`), and `TimeOrSrCaptureWinRule.cs:83-89` decides the **time-expiry winner** by `OrderByDescending(s => s.Value.Total)` (`:57-60` does the same for the SR-capture branch's runner-up). Flagging five infantry types therefore changes tournament **outcomes**, not just reported numbers. The claim that survives is narrower: nothing in the SIMULATION branches on ArmyValue (both victory-condition traits sort on `Experience`), so it is not a determinism or byte-identity concern — but on the harness it is scoring, and **any in-flight ladder baseline is invalidated**; pre- and post-change match scores must not be compared.
- **Arithmetic is a cheap way to refute "the selector is broken" before reading more code.** The census is per-mille of army VALUE and a mortar costs 300 (`infantry.yaml:1514`) against a 50‰ target, so 23 standing mortars (6900) would need a **138 000**-value army to sit at target. No plausible army is that large ⇒ either the deficit lane never governed those buys, or the observation isn't measuring what it claims. That one division redirected the search from the selector to the widget.
- **`ChooseByDeficit`'s "no opinion ⇒ fall back to the legacy picker" branch fires on BEING BROKE, and the legacy picker is the uniform lottery.** `IsCompositionCandidateEligible` ends with an affordability test (`CompositionNeedMath.Affordable(budget, cost, 100)`), so when `Cash + Resources` is below the cheapest composed unit **nothing is eligible**, `SelectDeficit` returns -1, and the caller draws `ChooseRandomUnitToBuild` — the lifetime-proportional lottery `CompositionDirected` exists to replace. `Order.StartProduction` queues regardless of cash, so that lottery buy really happens. An AI spends to zero routinely, so this is not a rare edge but a standing leak of the old drift back into the new lane — and it is invisible, because the composition log line only prints on the *successful* deficit path. The branch conflated "this queue has no composed type at all" (a heli-only pool — genuine no-opinion) with "composed types exist but are all priced out or capped" (a decision not to buy).
- **The external-request FIFO buys with NO caps at all.** `UnitBuilderBotModule.BuildUnit(bot, string name)` (`:538-564`, the single-name overload) issues `Order.StartProduction` without `UnitsToBuild`, `UnitDelays`, `UnitLimits`, the resupply/AA/transport gates, or any composition test. `CaptureCoordinatorBotModule.cs:710-712` documents that deliberately for the capturer floor — but `AdaptiveProductionBotModule` rides the **same** lane for its counter-buys (`at`/`aa`/`e3`/`ar`, `ai.yaml:694-696`), so counter demand could out-buy the composition targets without bound. On @experimental the capturer floor uses the PRIORITY lane instead (`TecnRequestPriority: true`, `ai.yaml:152`), which cleanly separates the two: the FIFO can be folded under the composition ceiling without touching the capture-supply floor.
- **"Only buy classes under target, else fall back to least-over" is a PROVABLE NO-OP over an argmax — and the safety fallback is what makes it vacuous.** Reasoning about the stall risk (a strict ceiling deadlocks when the only under-target classes are the `UnitLimits`-capped ones — sniper 3 / engineer 2 / technician 3 sitting under their small targets *at their cap* while everything else is a hair over) produced a prefer-under-target-with-least-over-fallback design that *felt* like a ceiling and shipped as one, until adversarial review re-derived it: let strict = argmax over `{eligible ∧ deficit > 0}` and loose = argmax over `{eligible}`, both tie-breaking to the lowest index. If any eligible slot has deficit > 0 then the loose maximizer itself has deficit > 0, so it lies in the strict candidate set, the maxima coincide, and every maximizer is in the strict set ⇒ the two agree exactly. If none does, strict returns −1 and the fallback runs loose. So `strict-then-loose ≡ loose`, unconditionally — including the very capped case the fallback was written for. **The property actually wanted ("an over-target class is never bought while any class is still short") was already a property of the plain argmax; nothing had to be added to get it.** Deleted rather than kept-and-documented. Two transferable shapes: (1) when you add a restriction AND a fallback that lifts it, check whether the pair collapses before writing the prose that claims otherwise — the fallback is the tell; (2) the no-op survived because it was reasoned about only in the failure case (does it stall?) and never in the success case (does it *differ*?).
- **A declined build cycle shifts the @experimental `LocalRandom` sequence.** The decline returns before `ChooseRandomUnitToBuild`, skipping the `.Random(world.LocalRandom)` draw the fallback would have made, so the draw sequence diverges from any pre-change @experimental baseline from the first declined cycle on. Sync-safe (every client runs the same code and the flag is config, not a runtime branch) and irrelevant to `@stable`/normal/rush/turtle, which never enter the path — but replays and seed-matched comparisons against older @experimental runs are no longer step-comparable.
- **`UnitLimits` is not a standing-count cap in a reinforcement model — it lags by the whole map-edge walk-in.** The check counts WORLD ACTORS only (`UnitBuilderBotModule.cs:393-396`, and the module's own note at `:499-503` already flags the queued-item blind spot), but under the Supply Route model a call-in spawns at the map edge and *walks* to the rally point, which takes far longer than the 30-tick purchase cycle. So a lane that picks the same type on consecutive cycles sees `count == 0` every time and can overshoot the cap by however many cycles fit inside the delivery time. The composition lane defends itself against exactly this with the pending-call-in credit in `CensusValues()` — `UnitLimits` has no such credit, and neither does the lottery fallback above. That is the most likely explanation for a standing engineer count well above `UnitLimits.e6.america: 2`: broke-cycle lottery draws, uncredited and uncapped. Worth remembering whenever a "limit" is read as a guarantee.
- **Head count and value share diverge hard when costs span 4x.** Rifle/LMG/grenadier cost 100, team leader 200, mortar/AT/AA 300, sniper 400 (`infantry.yaml`), so a per-VALUE target vector that *looks* balanced converts to a very different per-HEAD army. The pre-existing infantry block (e3 60 / ar 60 / tl 40 / e2 40 against at 90 / mt 50 / aa 40 / …) is 65% line infantry by head, not the ~80% the design intent implies. Anyone eyeballing `UnitTargetShares` is reading value; the thing being designed is heads. Convert before judging.
- **CORRECTION (post-merge verification, NIT-A) — "`Order.StartProduction` queues regardless of cash" named the wrong queue; the buy is real, but the queue is the bot's rate-limited ORDER buffer, and the ceiling's census coupling runs through request-list removal, not that buffer.** A module's `Order.StartProduction` goes through `IBot.QueueOrder`, which only does `orders.Enqueue(order)` (`ModularBot.cs:90-97` — the file lives in `Traits/Player/`); `ModularBot`'s `ITick.Tick` then drains the buffer rate-limited by `MinOrderQuotientPerTick` (~`:127-135`). So the lottery buy does land — the conclusion stands — but nothing about cash is decided at enqueue, and NO claim is recorded here about how the production side treats cash in the rebuilt call-in model (unverified; deliberately not asserted). The ceiling⇄census coupling is separate machinery: a ceiling-refused FIFO request is consumed EITHER WAY (`queuedBuildRequests.Remove(buildRequest)`, `UnitBuilderBotModule.cs:367`; contrast the priority lane's peek-don't-pop `:353-354`, which pops only on successful `BuildUnit`), which drops it from `RequestedProductionCount` (`:393-399`, counts both request lists) and hence from `CensusValues`' pending credit (`:756-759`). A refusal is thus a deferral owned by the REQUESTER — it re-issues on its own cycle — and the census correctly stops crediting a refused buy rather than holding it against the ceiling forever.
- **The ^TECN flag gap's blast radius includes the harness telemetry, not just the army bar and win-rule scoring.** Because `^TECN` had no `UpdatesPlayerStatistics` node at all, technicians were also absent from `AssetsValue` and the per-type alive tally — which feed the match-record emissions `assets_value` (`BotVsBotMatchWatcher.cs:566`) and `unit_type_stats` (`~:592-596`) plus the observer ASSETS label (`ObserverStatsLogic.cs:457`). Flagging the five templates therefore makes those SERIES non-comparable pre/post as well: the win-rule bullet above covers match scores, this one covers the telemetry columns a later analysis might diff across the change.

## 2026-08-05 — Item 31 (aggressiveness slider + opportunistic advance, `auto/aggro-slider`): **the frontier-distance BFS makes "never walk into believed-enemy ground" structural for the ANCHOR — and the three round-1 review defects were all the same mistake, a property proved about one step and then assumed for the next**

> **[rejected: adversarial-review changelog for a default-off `@experimental` lever (`OpportunisticAdvanceMath`) plus slider-sweep methodology — size-a-slope-against-the-sweep-grid, clamp the knob domain, a scalar damper over a quantised anchor is a step-selector. Real discipline, but it belongs with the §2.6/§2.7 design doc and the sweep plan, not in engine/gameplay reference; the levers are off in every shipped profile. The one general engine fact — `ControlField.FrontierDistanceAt` is seeded from `Classify == Enemy` cells, so frontier distance 0 IS believed-enemy ground and an ownership-terminated descent provably halts on our side of the contour — is already banked in influence-stack.md §@experimental consumers (frontier-distance standoff).]** (curation 2026-08-06).

Built `OpportunisticAdvanceMath` (design §2.6) plus the slider hardening, wired as a second consumer of the free-pool staging walk in `PoiOffensiveBotModule.StageFreePool`. Adversarial review round 1 confirmed merge-safety at defaults but found four defects, three of which share a shape worth more than any of them individually: **a guarantee established at one stage of a pipeline was carried into the next stage where its precondition no longer held** — the walk's safety assumed for the fan-out (FIX 1), the abort's re-derivation assumed to reach the order despite a damper (FIX 2), the slope's live range assumed to be visible at the sweep points (FIX 3). Each correction is recorded inline below the claim it corrects, rather than by rewriting the original — the pairing is the lesson.

Findings:

- **`ControlField.FrontierDistanceAt` is seeded from `Classify == Enemy` cells, so frontier distance 0 IS believed-enemy ground — which means a descent that rejects Enemy-classified cells provably halts on OUR side of the frontline contour, with no distance or leash term doing the work.** This is why the advance can reuse `ForwardStagingMath`'s gradient wholesale instead of needing its own "how deep is too deep" geometry: `ForwardStagingMath.StagingCell` stops early by an explicit standoff (`PoiOffensiveBotModule.cs:1838-1842`), while `OpportunisticAdvanceMath.AdvanceCell` removes the standoff and lets the *ownership* condition terminate the walk. Two different behaviours, one field, no new sensor. Generalises: any "how far forward may I go" question over this stack is answerable by a gradient walk with a per-cell admissibility predicate, and the enemy-ownership term is always the backstop.
- **CORRECTION (review round 1, FIX 1) — that guarantee covers the ANCHOR, not the units, and the gap is a whole sector. A path-planner's safety property does not survive the formation step unless the formation step is tested too.** The walk emits one cell; `StageFreePool` then fans the screen around it with `ForwardStagingMath.SpreadCell` at `StagingSpreadStepCells: 2`, and with `ControlField.CellSize: 2` **one spread ring is exactly one coarse sector**. A depth-maximising walk routinely terminates at frontier distance 1 — one sector short of the enemy classification — so several octants of ring 1 land at distance 0, i.e. inside the very ground the walk refused, and the proof says nothing about them. The root cause is a contract inherited without its precondition: `StableSlot` documents (`ForwardStagingMath.cs:62-64`) that the caller must bound `maxRings` by the standoff so slots stay behind the cleared frontier — and the advance deleted the standoff, which was the whole point. Fixed by grant-testing each spread slot (`ResolveAdvanceAnchor`'s `slotGranted` out-param) and collapsing a failing slot onto the anchor. **General shape: when you reuse a helper after removing the guard its contract assumes, the contract is void, not merely weakened — re-establish it explicitly or the helper's own docs will read as reassurance.** Read-the-results caveat that falls out of the fix: the collapse stacks all failing forward octants on the anchor cell, so at ring 1 with the anchor at frontier distance 1 the screen's effective FRONTAGE is much narrower than its unit count suggests — force cap and frontage decouple.
- **FOLLOW-ON (review round 2, FIX A) — "one definition, so they can never drift apart" was itself untrue when written, and that class of claim is worth distrusting on sight.** The round-1 fix introduced a single `Granted()` closure and a comment saying all three consumers shared it; in fact only two did. `AdvanceCell` was still handed four *separately bound* lambdas, because its signature deliberately takes the conditions apart so the walk stays NUnit-pinnable without a World. The two bindings were verified term-by-term identical, so there was no behavioural defect — but the comment asserted an invariant the code did not enforce, i.e. precisely the reassurance the remedy was supposed to provide. Fixed by hoisting the four samplers to named locals (`EnemyAt`/`ContactAt`/`DangerAt`/`PassableAt`) and building both `Granted()` and the `AdvanceCell` argument list from those same objects, so sameness is now structural. **General shape: "single source of truth" comments should name the object that is shared. If you cannot point at one, the sentence is an intention, not an invariant — and a reviewer will read it as a proof that was never done.**
- **The staging descent's `dangerField != null ? … : 0` fallback (`PoiOffensiveBotModule.cs:1841`, mirrored in `ResolveMusterAnchor`) is safe ONLY because staging has an independent brake, and it is a live trap for anything that extends the walk.** For the muster point the standoff still holds the reserve behind the line whether or not a danger field exists, so reading "no field ⇒ danger 0" costs nothing. Remove the standoff — as an advance must — and that same fallback silently converts the danger ceiling into a blank cheque: every cell passes the low-danger condition and the screen walks into any envelope. The advance therefore treats a missing danger field as **disqualifying**, not as zero danger (`ResolveAdvanceAnchor`'s `fieldsAvailable` term). **General shape: a permissive null-field fallback is a property of the CONSUMER's other guards, not of the field — re-derive it when reusing the sampler under different guards.**
- **A behaviour whose target is re-derived from scratch every evaluation needs no abort path — and gets a strictly better one for free.** §2.6 specifies that an Advance "aborts the instant a believed contact or danger spike appears in the corridor, degrading gracefully to a Hold at the newly-reached line." Implemented as re-derivation that costs zero code: a contact appearing in the corridor removes those cells from the next walk, the walk returns a shallower cell (or the seed ⇒ no advance), the unit's target cell differs from what it was last sent to, and the existing `stagedCells` dedup re-issues it rearward. No PER-UNIT mode flag is needed — the target cell carries it. Contrast the retreat FSM (`CombatRetreatMath` + `RetreatDamperMath`), which needs explicit dwell/hysteresis precisely because it carries a mode. **Prefer re-derivation over a mode flag when the decision is cheap and its inputs are already recomputed on a cadence.**
- **CORRECTION (review round 1, FIX 2) — the original claim "the only state is the target cell" was false, and the state it overlooked was silently disabling the very abort the bullet above celebrates.** `lastAdvanceAnchor` (`PoiOffensiveBotModule.cs`) is module state, and it fed a *symmetric* Chebyshev hysteresis copied from staging. **The arithmetic makes that copy actively wrong: anchors are grid-cell CENTRES spaced `CellSize` apart, so a one-sector closure moves the candidate by exactly 2 map cells — below the then-shipped `AdvanceHysteresisCells: 3` (now 0, see the follow-on below) — and the abort was therefore suppressed in precisely the single-sector case it exists for**, leaving the screen ordered at ground that had just failed the grant test. Re-derivation is only as good as your willingness to ACT on the re-derivation. Fixed with a one-way rule (`OpportunisticAdvanceMath.AdoptAdvanceAnchor`): hysteresis may damp a deepening or lateral move, but a held anchor that stops passing the grant test, or a walk that no longer reaches as far, is adopted immediately. **The asymmetry with staging is the durable lesson: a stale STAGING anchor is still safe ground (chosen a standoff behind the line), so symmetric damping there is free; a stale ADVANCE anchor is a claim about ground being free that has expired. Damping a signal is safe only where being late is harmless — check that before copying a damper across consumers.**
- **FOLLOW-ON (review round 2, FIX B) — the one-way rule was correct and still left the knob unusable, because a scalar threshold is ill-posed against a QUANTISED anchor. `AdvanceHysteresisCells` is now 0, and the honest finding is that it has no good non-zero value at all.** Fixing the direction exposed the units problem underneath: the anchor is a grid-cell CENTRE, so it only ever moves in whole sectors (`CellSize` map cells). A threshold under one sector damps nothing; a threshold at or above one sector blocks a one-sector **re-deepening** — and the one-way rule removed the escape, because the blocked move is no longer a delay. **An ordinary damper is a delay only because the suppressed move eventually wins on a later pass; here the identical one-sector gap recurs every eval, so "damped" means "refused forever", and the advance ratchets to the shallowest depth it has ever reached** — silently biasing effective depth downward and confounding the depth dial FIX 3 had just made legible. Expressing the threshold in grid cells does not rescue it (1 sector admits every move ⇒ equals 0; 2 sectors re-creates the block), which is what makes 0 a considered answer rather than a disabled knob. **General shape: before adding a magnitude threshold, check the quantum of the thing being measured — if the quantity moves in steps, a scalar threshold is a step-selector, not a damper. And a damper over a re-derived-from-scratch signal must be checked for whether the suppressed move can ever recur differently; if it cannot, damping is refusal.** If lateral jitter ever measures as a real cost the fix is to split the test by direction (damp lateral, never damp depth), not to raise a scalar. Order churn on a static anchor was never this knob's job anyway — the per-unit target-cell dedup in `StageFreePool` does that, whatever the adopt rule returns. Side note worth keeping, because two people in a row assumed the opposite: **`ForwardStagingMath.AnchorShifted` is true for EVERY displacement at a non-positive threshold, including zero — it is not a "did it actually move?" test.** The mechanism is that the comparison is *vacuous* there, not that the early return at `ForwardStagingMath.cs:169-170` intercepts it: Chebyshev distance is never negative, so `Math.Max(|dx|,|dy|) >= thresholdCells` holds for any displacement once the threshold is ≤ 0. The early return states that explicitly but is behaviourally redundant — deleting it changes nothing, which also means no test can pin it. **Round-3 review caught this being mis-stated as "an early return, NOT a displacement test", i.e. crediting the guard clause with a property the arithmetic already had; the pin was on the right observable but the explanation named the wrong cause. Worth generalising: when a function has a short-circuit AND a general path that agree, do not attribute the behaviour to the short-circuit without checking what the general path would have returned.**
- **How a slider ships "inert" determines whether it can ever be swept — slope-0 inertness and gate inertness are NOT interchangeable.** Phase 1d's `Aggressiveness` rides `MissionCommitmentEnabled`, which is ON in `@experimental` (`rules/ai/ai.yaml`), so its only guarantee of no behaviour change was `MissionBetterOppMarginSlopePct: 0` — i.e. the knob provably moves nothing, and sweeping it requires first *guessing* a slope, folding a second unmeasured variable into the sweep. A knob that instead rides its OWN default-off gate can ship **live** slopes with real declared ranges, so flipping the gate yields a slider that already spans a designed interval and the sweep has exactly one variable. Item 31's three advance dials are built that way deliberately. **When adding the remaining §2.7 knobs (`RiskTolerance`, `CaptureVsCombatPriority`), give each a gate of its own rather than parking it slope-0 behind an already-enabled lever.**
- **CORRECTION (review round 1, FIX 3) — "the slope is live" is not the same as "the dial moves", and integer truncation is where the difference hides. SIZE A SLOPE AGAINST THE SWEEP GRID, NOT THE ADVERTISED ENDPOINTS.** The shift is `(knob − 50) · slope / 100` in C# integer division (truncating toward zero), so a knob 15 points off neutral needs `slope ≥ 7` to move an integer dial *at all* — `15·6/100 = 0`. The first cut shipped depth slope 4 and force slope 6 and advertised ranges 1→5 and 2→8; those ranges were real but reachable only at knob 0/100, **off the parked sweep grid**. On the grid `{20,35,50,65,80}` the dials read depth `{2,3,3,3,4}` and force `{4,5,5,5,6}` — three of five points identical to neutral — so the planned five-point sweep would have varied one dial (the danger ceiling, whose slope 40 was coarse enough) while appearing to vary three. **A slider can be live, documented, correctly clamped, NUnit-pinned at its endpoints and still be inert everywhere it is actually going to be measured.** Slopes widened to 7; the grid now gives depth `{1,2,3,4,5}` and force `{3,4,5,6,7}`, and the on-grid movement is now itself pinned (`Dials_MoveAtEveryPointOfTheParkedSweepGrid`) rather than only the endpoints. Widening is safe by construction — `(50−50)·slope/100 = 0` for any slope, so neutral is exact and no baseline moves. **Rule for the remaining knobs: pin the dial's value at every planned sweep point, not at 0 and 100.**
- **`PoiOffenseMath.ShiftByKnob` had no domain guard, and a swept knob is exactly the input most likely to arrive out of range.** Every §2.7 slider is one multiplication away from a threshold, so an out-of-range sweep point (a harness typo, an off-by-one in a grid definition) previously ran straight past the intended extreme into behaviour no slider setting was meant to produce — silently, since nothing validates `Info` ints. Clamping inside the single seam (`ClampKnob`, 0..100) makes a bad sweep point degenerate to the cautious or reckless extreme, which is a legible result. In-domain values pass through untouched, so no shipped config changes.

## 2026-08-04 — Supply-hunt T2 close-out (merged @ `2dc5c8ee`): the per-owner-class OFF switch for `AutoSeekSupplies` is a mechanical conversion away, and reviewer REPLACEMENT PROSE needs the same adjudication as implementer code

> **[rejected: per-branch disposition note (the not-taken per-owner-class gate for `AutoSeekSupplies` — `TraitInfo` → `ConditionalTraitInfo` + `GrantConditionOnBotOwner` is the standard in-tree idiom, not a discovery) plus review-process methodology (“replacement prose transmitted through a review is unreviewed code”). The durable arithmetic fact underneath the prose dispute — `WVec.HorizontalLength` floors via `Exts.ISqrt`, so a squared comparison is the honest boundary test — is banked with the aura-range fix → economy.md §What rearms what.]** (curation 2026-08-06).

- **`AutoSeekSupplies` shipped default-ON for every owner (`infantry.yaml` `^Soldier`, isolated commit `f15cfbde` — revert it alone to undo) and has NO per-owner-class gate: `AutoSeekSuppliesInfo` derives from plain `TraitInfo`, so it cannot be condition-disabled.** If live play wants the flip bot-only (or human-only), the conversion is mechanical and needs no new engine machinery: `TraitInfo` → `ConditionalTraitInfo`, an `IsTraitDisabled` bail at the top of `TickIdle`, and grant the condition per owner class via `GrantConditionOnBotOwner` / `GrantConditionOnHumanOwner` — both traits already exist in the tree. Left out of T2 deliberately: the posted fork-record default was ON-for-everyone, and the stance toggle already gives humans a per-unit opt-out.
- **A reviewer's replacement text can smuggle in a NEW false claim — check the checker.** T2's anti-stall proof was repaired twice: the original comment argued in a circle (transposed the 300-wu bound and presupposed cell distinctness), and the reviewer's own replacement sketch asserted the truncated stop point "can only shorten / lies at most `stop`" — false: `WVec.HorizontalLength` is a FLOOR integer sqrt, so it UNDERSTATES `distance`, OVERSTATES the scale `stop/distance`, and pushes the scaled point OUTWARD (net strictly < `stop + 1`; the honest truck-distance bound is > 1023, not > 1024) — contradicting the same reviewer's round-2 bound. Both clauses were caught only by manager merge-gate diff-reads of prose-only commits (`5012650a`, `5f1af3aa`). Rule: replacement prose transmitted through a review is unreviewed code until someone re-derives it; a comment stating a bound must name the direction of every rounding step it leans on.

## 2026-08-04 — Supply-hunt Tier 2 (idle-truck hunt, `auto/supply-t2`): **`InfluenceStack.Participates` stopped being a profile discriminator on 2026-08-02, and every in-file comment that still calls it one is now wrong** — a shared module's "@experimental-only" flags reach `@stable`

> **[promoted (partial): → influence-stack.md §Invariants — the second gating pattern REWRITTEN on sight, because the doc itself carried the now-false advice. Re-verified: `InfluenceStack.Participates` returns true for `experimental` OR `stable` (and every human combatant) at `InfluenceStack.cs:43-52`, so an `Info.Flag && Participates(player)` double-gate does NOT preserve `@stable` byte-identity; the house answer is `CommitOnOrderMath.ShouldCommitShared(flag, resource, isExperimentalBot)` (`PoiGoalGuard.cs:300-301`). The stale-comment hazard and the three older `SupplyFollowerBotModule` flags that genuinely reach `@stable` are recorded there. ALSO → economy.md §What rearms what: the structural truck-cannot-serve-a-vehicle split (verified, and sharpened — even at the LC the *push* reaches only `himars`/`iskander`, the two vehicles declaring `replenish-vehicles`; no `RearmActors` anywhere names `supplycache`, so a cache is push-only) and the unbounded Hunt-stance provider scan. NOT banked: the `clusters.Count == 0` loop-shape observation (single-site review note).]** (curation 2026-08-06).

Built `SupplyTruckHuntMath` + `SupplyFollowerBotModule.HuntStarvingInfantry`. Findings:

- **The `participates` double-gate no longer isolates `@stable`, and the code says it does.** `InfluenceStack.Participates` returns true for `BotType == experimental || BotType == stable` (`InfluenceStack.cs:47-48`) since the 0802 parity promotion — `ai.yaml:1335-1337` states the consequence explicitly ("@stable now gets them automatically … via the widened participation"). But `SupplyFollowerBotModule`'s own field comment still reads "*every new @experimental behaviour is double-gated on `participates` so @stable/Normal/Rush/Turtle stay byte-identical*" (`SupplyFollowerBotModule.cs:149-151`), and `ai.yaml:657-658` repeats it for `SectorSpread` / `SmallSquadCoverage` / `DangerEvac`. Both were TRUE when written. **They are now load-bearing false comments sitting on the exact seam a reviewer checks byte-identity at.** The house answer already exists — `CommitOnOrderMath.ShouldCommitShared(flag, resource, isExperimentalBot)` (`PoiGoalGuard.cs:300`) was written for precisely this, and its doc-comment is the only place in the tree that states the hazard correctly; `GarrisonBotModule` consumes it via `player.BotType == InfluenceStack.ExperimentalBotType` (`:102`). Tier 2's hunt uses that explicit gate rather than `participates`. **General shape: a gate whose meaning is defined elsewhere can be widened out from under every comment that describes it — when a comment asserts byte-identity, re-derive the gate instead of trusting the sentence.** (NOT fixed here: the three older flags above genuinely do reach `@stable` now. That is either an accepted consequence of the promotion or an unnoticed leak; it predates this lane and reverting it would change live `@stable` behaviour, so it is reported, not touched.)
- **A supply TRUCK cannot resupply a vehicle at all — the split is structural, not a tuning choice.** `SupplyProvider`'s push is gated on the recipient holding the provider's `RearmCondition` (`SupplyProvider.cs:488-494`). TRUK and SUPPLYCACHE both use `replenish-soldiers` (`vehicles.yaml:546`, `misc.yaml:412`), an `ExternalCondition` carried only by `^Soldier` (`infantry.yaml:214-215`). The **only** provider using `replenish-vehicles` is the static `logisticscenter` (`structures.yaml:394`), and it is additionally `DockedCondition: unit.docked` — so vehicles must PULL from a fixed building and can never be served in the field. Any "supply truck follows the army" reasoning that counts vehicle ammo as demand is counting demand the truck cannot relieve: `SupplyFollowerBotModule`'s cluster `AmmoNeed` sums **every** `AmmoPool` in the cluster (`:519-522`), vehicles included, so a purely-armour cluster still attracts a truck that can do nothing for it. (Left alone — changing the cluster score changes live behaviour for every profile; the Tier 2 sweep filters demand by the condition instead.)
- **There is already an UNBOUNDED whole-map truck hunt in the engine, one stance away from live.** `SupplyProvider.UpdateTarget` falls through to `FindNeedsResupplyTarget` when `AutoTarget.EngagementStanceValue >= EngagementStance.Hunt` (`:302-305`), and that helper scans `world.ActorsHavingTrait<AmmoPool>()` with **no range term whatsoever** (`:356-364`); `SetTarget` then drives the provider to it (`:513-525`). It is dormant by default (TRUK's `AutoTarget` block overrides only `InitialResupplyBehavior*`, `vehicles.yaml:514-516`, so it ships `Defensive` — the engine default for both the human and AI fields, `AutoTarget.cs:160/163`), but any player or module that sets a truck to Hunt gets cross-map wandering with no leash. Tier 2 deliberately did **not** extend this path — it is a second, parallel hunt, and the bounded one belongs where the bot can see the whole sector. **Worth knowing before someone "enables truck hunting" by flipping a stance.**
- **`clusters.Count == 0` guarding a `break` inside a `foreach` is a loop-invariant test wearing a per-iteration costume** (`SupplyFollowerBotModule.cs:289`). It reads like "skip this truck" but means "never enter the loop" — so bolting a per-truck fallback onto the `continue` paths alone silently misses the single most important idle case: **no clusters at all**, i.e. every truck idle. Byte-identity is preserved by keeping the `break` on the flag-off path, not by leaving the shape alone.

## 2026-08-04 — Wave B (SR pooling, `auto/waves-ab`): three independent causes, and the unifying lesson is that **every one of them was a "wait" with no terminating condition** — an absolute strength bar a small axis can never clear, a fall-back destination that resolves to "where you already are", and a unit class no movement module will accept

> **[promoted (partial): → influence-stack.md §Frontline reachability & staging — P3 damper bullet gains the two general rules (*a gate comparing a per-axis quantity to a global constant needs a proof the quantity can reach it, or it is a permanent veto wearing the costume of a delay*; *“bounded per occurrence” is not bounded — check what resets the counter*), and the P7 muster bullet gains the self-seed-has-THREE-meanings guard, the null-beats-a-useless-value rule for `??` chains, and the grid-space parity requirement (`GridCellToMapCell` returns the cell CENTRE, so a map-space round-trip only reproduces odd coordinates at `CellSize: 2`). ALSO → architecture.md §The bot free pool self-heals: SHORAD is orphaned by a double exclusion and bought anyway — verify the module that supposedly “owns” an excluded class actually lists it (`FoldShortRangeAdIntoLine`, off even in `@experimental`, `PoiOffensiveBotModule.cs:2269`). The entry's own note that `influence-stack.md`'s line refs had drifted was correct and has drifted FURTHER — `ResolveMusterAnchor` is now `:2070`, not `:1717`; the doc is corrected. NOT banked: per-wave flag state and the test-fixture note.]** (curation 2026-08-06).

Reshaped `RetreatDamperMath`'s strength gate, guarded `ResolveMusterAnchor`, and added a default-off SHORAD line-fold. Each cause was verified in code first; the line refs in `influence-stack.md` had drifted (`:1619-1632` is now `SelectStickyTargets`; `ResolveMusterAnchor` is at `:1717`).

- **An ABSOLUTE strength floor is unreachable for an axis the allocator never funds that high — so "wait for mass" became "never advance".** `MinAdvanceStrength: 1200` is on the health-weighted build-value scale, but `AllocateProportional` may only ever fund an axis to 2-3 hulls (~900), so `BelowAdvanceStrength` stayed true for the whole match and the axis parked in the rally bubble. The 2026-08-03 diagnosis below already measured this firing (34 `[exp-retreat] fallback` events with **zero** `[exp-retreat] state` lines ⇒ every one was damper-hold `(b)`, never a genuine retreat). The reshape reads the floor as fill-completion — hold only while the force the allocator ALLOCATED has not all arrived — plus a `MaxAdvanceHoldEvals` cap as the backstop for an axis being starved. **General shape: a gate that compares a per-axis quantity against a global constant needs a proof that the quantity can ever reach the constant; if it cannot, the gate is a permanent veto wearing the costume of a delay.** Both new conditions deliberately fail OPEN (toward advancing), because parking was the failure mode.
- **A "fall back to somewhere better" helper that can return the caller's CURRENT position both no-ops the order and swallows the fall-back chain.** `ResolveMusterAnchor` seeds `ForwardStagingMath.StagingCell` from the axis's own unit centroid; on a flat/unpopulated frontier field the descent finds no improving neighbour and returns the seed. The caller is `OrderRetreat(bot, axis, ResolveMusterAnchor(axis) ?? stagingAnchor ?? rallyCell.Value, tick)` — so a non-null self-seed means the `??` chain **never runs**, and the axis is ordered to regroup where it already stands. Near the SR that is the pooling symptom exactly. `ResolveStagingAnchor` has the equivalent guard (`candidate == rallyCell.Value`); the per-axis twin was missing it. **When a resolver feeds a `??` fall-back chain, "returns a useless value" is strictly worse than "returns null" — the useless value also disables every fall-back behind it.**
- **The guard has to be written in GRID space, not map space.** `ControlField.GridCellToMapCell` returns the grid cell CENTRE (`gx * CellSize + CellSize / 2`), so with `CellSize: 2` the map-space round-trip reproduces the input only for ODD coordinates (`x/2*2+1 == x`) and lands one cell off for even ones. A map-space `candidate == seed` test would therefore miss the no-move case half the time. (Noted in passing: `ResolveStagingAnchor`'s existing map-space comparison carries this same parity dependence — left alone, out of scope, but it is a latent wrinkle.)
- **SHORAD is orphaned by a DOUBLE exclusion, and it is bought anyway.** `AdaptiveProductionBotModule.AntiAirRoles` is exactly `{ ShortRangeAD }`, so the bot purchases mobile AA — but (1) every ground movement module filters the free pool to `MainBattle`/`IndirectFire` (`PoiOffensiveBotModule`, `PoiGarrisonBotModule`, `LayeredDefenceBotModule.IsLineEligibleByRole`), and (2) `strykershorad` additionally trips the `!IsTroopCarrier` term because it has `Cargo: MaxWeight: 9`, and (3) it is **not** in `MountedTransportBotModule`'s `CarrierTypes` (`bradley, bmp2, m113`), so the carrier module does not own it either. Result: bought, never moved, stands at the Supply Route all match. The comment claiming cargo carriers "stay owned by MountedTransportBotModule" is true for the IFVs and **false for SHORAD** — the exclusion assumes an owner that the YAML never assigned. **When excluding a class from a pool "because another module owns it", verify that module's config actually lists it.**
- **The SHORAD fold ships OFF even in `@experimental`** (matching the fires-batch precedent below): folding air defence onto an offensive axis moves the air cover off the rear, which is a doctrine A/B, not a bug fix. Correct-but-dark is the deliberate state; the *finding* is the deliverable.

**CORRECTION 2026-08-04 (adversarial review ⇒ fix commit).** Two of the above fixes were themselves defective, and both defects share a shape worth more than either fix: **a guard is only as good as its case analysis, and a budget is only a bound if it cannot be refunded.**

- **The self-seed guard treated three different situations as one.** `ForwardStagingMath.StagingCell` returns its seed in THREE cases: the axis is already at the standoff (`ForwardStagingMath.cs:97-98`), every forward neighbour is danger-blocked so the walk breaks at step 0 (`:116-117` + `:128-129`), or the field is flat. Only the third — near the SR — is the pooling pathology; the first two ARE the designed Phase-7 "hold in place at the line" contract, which the surviving doc comment still described. Nulling all three inverted the lever for forward axes: null falls through to `?? stagingAnchor ?? rallyCell`, a grouped AttackMove REARWARD, and the posture-hold that consumes this fires *only* on a forward axis in contact (`FrontlineAllocationMath.SectorPostureHold` requires `frontierEdges > 0` + an enemy present, `:195-198`) — i.e. precisely cases 1/2. So the guard manufactured the rearward flow the wave existed to remove. Now gated on `axis.NearRally`: near ⇒ null (pull forward), forward ⇒ return the axis's own cell so `OrderRetreat`'s `moved` guard suppresses the order and it holds. **Lesson: when a helper returns "the input" as a sentinel, enumerate every path that produces it before attaching one meaning to it.** (Rejected alternative: discriminating on `FrontierDistanceAt >= MaxFrontierDistanceCells` — a populated field near the SR would re-freeze the rear axis, reintroducing case 3.)
- **`MaxAdvanceHoldEvals` was a duty-cycle limiter, not a terminating condition.** `StepFillHold(held, holding) => holding ? held + 1 : 0` zeroed the counter on the RELEASE eval, refunding the budget the instant it was spent — so a still-near-rally, still-under-filled axis re-parked on the next eval and ping-ponged hold x 6 / advance x 1 indefinitely, issuing a fall-back order on every hold. It appeared to terminate only because `MissionCommitmentWindowTicks` usually carried the released axis out of the rally bubble; it does NOT for an axis whose objective sits inside the bubble or that is pinned in contact near its own SR — the "small movements near spawn" symptom exactly. The budget is now spent once per massing EPISODE (`StepFillHold(held, holding, stillMassing)`, persisting across the release while `nearRally && FillIncomplete`), so the release is sticky. **Lesson: "bounded per occurrence" is not "bounded" — check what resets the counter, and whether the reset can be reached by the very condition the bound exists to escape.**
- **The fixture flip that hid it.** The original sequence test set `nearRally = false` on the release eval to "model the axis advancing away", which made the ping-pong unobservable. It now holds `nearRally` true for the whole run — the worst case — and asserts a re-park is a failure. Verified by construction: reverting `StepFillHold` to the old reset makes exactly this pin (plus its unit-level twin) go red. **A sequence fixture that helpfully advances the world past the bug proves the bug cannot be seen, not that it is absent.**

## 2026-08-04 — Wave A (out-of-ammo disposition + supply-depot capture tier, `auto/waves-ab`, default-off): **a FILTER is not a DISPOSITION** — `SkipOutOfAmmoUnits` stops the bot recruiting a dry unit but never gives it anything to do, and the engine-side fallback it relies on is flag-only

> **[promoted (partial): → economy.md §What rearms what — “a resupplier EXISTS” is the engine's whole reachability test (`ChooseResupplier` ends in `ClosestToIgnoringPath`, `AmmoPool.cs:343-344`, no path and no `IsInWorld` check); `AutoRearmIfAllEmpty` requires EVERY pool empty (`:170-173`); `ResupplyBehavior: Evacuate` is opt-in per actor; and `MobileInfo.LocomotorInfo.SharesCell` as the Info-level infantry/vehicle discriminator (`Mobile.cs:143-154`; `SharesCell: true` on exactly the four `foot*` locomotors, `world.yaml:32/47/64/80` — caveat: `Walker` `:193` does not share cells). **TWO CLAIMS ARE WRONG and are banked corrected.** (1) “the no-source branch is FLAG-ONLY … a flag no consumer reads” — `NeedsResupply` has two live readers, `SupplyProvider.cs:361` (Hunt-stance scan) and `UnitBuilderBotModule.AnyFieldedUnitNeedsResupply` `:487` (a production gate); neither disposes of the unit, so the *conclusion* survives but “nothing reads it” is false. (2) “only TRUK-family vehicles override to `Evacuate`” — m270 (`vehicles-america.yaml:704`), grad (`vehicles-russia.yaml:529`) and tos (`vehicles-russia.yaml:652`) do too. NOT banked: the capture-weight tier (config change) and the `evacuatingOutOfAmmo` pruning wrinkle (→ bugs/discovered.md shape, and the entry already records it as pre-existing).]** (curation 2026-08-06).

Built `AmmoEvacMath` + `PoiOffensiveBotModule.SweepOutOfAmmoUnits` + the `CaptureSupplyDepots` weight tier. Findings:

- **`AmmoPool.AutoRearm`'s no-source branch is FLAG-ONLY, and nothing answers the flag.** When `ChooseResupplier` returns null, `AutoRearm` sets `NeedsResupply = true` on every pool and returns (`AmmoPool.cs:313-320`), with an explicit comment that "Evacuation only happens when ResupplyBehavior is explicitly set to Evacuate". Ground vehicles default to `ResupplyBehavior.Auto` (`defaults.yaml:318-319`; only TRUK-family vehicles override to `Evacuate`), so a dry vehicle with no Logistics Centre raises a flag no consumer reads and keeps fighting with an empty gun. The unit-side trait *cannot* fix this — "is a source worth driving to, and if not do we cut our losses?" is a judgement about the sector, so it belongs in the bot module. **General shape: when a trait's fallback is to set a field, grep for the field's readers before assuming the fallback does anything.**
- **`SkipOutOfAmmoUnits` and "evacuate the dry unit" are easy to conflate and are not the same mechanism.** The existing flag is a *recruit filter* (keeps an evacuating unit off an axis so the `AttackMove` doesn't cancel its `RotateToEdge`). It presupposes an evac that something else started. Nothing started one for ground vehicles. The two are complementary, not alternatives — which is why the new lever documents that it *needs* `SkipOutOfAmmoUnits` to be useful.
- **`AmmoPool.ChooseResupplier` does NOT path-check** — it ends in `ClosestToIgnoringPath` (`AmmoPool.cs:343-344`), filtering only ownership / `RearmActors` membership / remaining supply. So "a reachable source exists" in engine terms means "a source EXISTS", and a depot across an unfordable river reads as reachable. Any consumer that wants reachability must supply its own proxy (here: a Chebyshev seek budget, documented as a proxy rather than dressed up as a path test).
- **`MobileInfo.LocomotorInfo.SharesCell` is a name-free, Info-level infantry/vehicle discriminator.** `MobileInfo.LocomotorInfo` is resolved in `RulesetLoaded` (`Mobile.cs:143`), so it is readable from `ActorInfo` with no trait instance and no TraitDictionary lookup. In WW3MOD `SharesCell: true` appears on exactly the four `foot*` locomotors and no vehicle locomotor (`world.yaml`), so it cleanly separates "topped up in place by a truck/cache PUSH" (infantry) from "must drive to a depot" (vehicles) without an actor-name list — the case-mismatch hazard `ActorNameCase` exists to paper over. There was no existing `IsInfantry`/`IsVehicle` helper in the bot layer; this is the cheapest correct one.
- **A capture-priority table with a flat default silently equalises very different targets.** `logisticscenter` was ALREADY in `CapturableActorTypes` (`ai.yaml:103`) but absent from `IncomeWeights`, so it scored `DefaultIncomeWeight: 10` — tied with the no-income MISS, below HOSP (20). The only rearm host a ground vehicle has ranked level with a decorative civilian building. The fix is a weight tier, not a membership change. **When auditing a "priorities" config, the interesting entries are the ones NOT listed** — they are all silently equal.
- **`RotateToEdge`'s fixed-refund path matches economy.md exactly**: `refund = fixedRefund x hp / maxHP` (`RotateToEdge.cs:275-280`), so `GetSellValue()` + `RotateToEdge` is the whole evac economy and needed no new arithmetic. Ammo is a small share of a tank's cost, so an empty tank still refunds most of its budget — which is *why* terminal evac (rather than a park-and-recheck loop) is the right disposition when no depot exists.
- **CORRECTION 2026-08-04 (review):** an earlier draft of this work claimed a passing truck could top a vehicle up mid-evac. **It cannot.** The provider PUSH is gated on the recipient carrying the provider's `RearmCondition` external condition — default `replenish-soldiers` (`SupplyProvider.cs:59`, looked up on the recipient at `:431-434`) — and that condition is declared as an `ExternalCondition` only on infantry (`infantry.yaml:215`). `vehicles.yaml:546` is TRUK's own `RearmCondition` *field* (the provider naming its gate), not a vehicle declaring the condition, which is what made the grep misleading. A vehicle can only ever PULL (drive to a host). The `evacuatingOutOfAmmo` set is still the right shape, but for a different reason: it records a DISPOSITION, and the ammo predicate it complements sits behind a separate flag (`SkipOutOfAmmoUnits`) that may be off.
